I Went Looking for Codex Auto Mode. It's Called 'Approve for Me' Now

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 10 • Originally published at its-tahir.com

I kept seeing people ask where Codex's auto mode is, the way Claude Code has one. It turns out there's no setting by that name: the closest thing is Approve for me (Auto-review), and the old --full-auto flag is gone. I went through the Codex docs, the release notes and the 0.166.0 package to map what each mode actually does. Are you running Codex with a reviewer, or still approving everything yourself?

OpenAI's Codex (the CLI, the desktop app and the IDE extension) has no setting called "auto mode", as of Codex 0.166.0. What people mean is Approve for me, which the docs also call Auto-review: Codex keeps the same workspace-write sandbox as its default mode, but when it needs to cross that sandbox, a separate reviewer agent approves or denies the request instead of you. Turn it on with codex --approve-for-me, with approvals_reviewer = "auto_review" in ~/.codex/config.toml, or by choosing Approve for me in the permissions control under the composer. The old --full-auto flag is gone: 0.147.0 removed it from codex exec, and the 0.166.0 build has no trace of it.

Last reviewed 5 November 2026, against Codex 0.166.0. Settings and behavior are from OpenAI's Codex docs and the release notes; flag names and on-screen text are quoted from the 0.166.0 Linux package, which I downloaded from npm and searched without running it. I couldn't run a signed-in Codex session for this post, so nothing here claims a first-hand test of the reviewer's decisions.

Approve for me changes who reviews a request to cross the sandbox. It doesn't widen the sandbox or turn on network access.

Not by that name, but Approve for me does the job. Codex has two separate controls. The sandbox decides what Codex can do on its own (read files, edit the workspace, use the network). The approval policy decides when it has to stop and ask. In the default mode, called Ask for approval in the app and Default in the 0.166.0 CLI picker, Codex edits files and runs commands inside your workspace without asking, and asks you before it crosses that boundary. OpenAI's approvals and security page calls this same default the Auto preset, which is part of the confusion: that preset is not Approve for me, because you still answer every request to cross the sandbox.

Approve for me keeps all of that and changes one thing: who answers. From the Auto-review docs:

Auto-review replaces manual approval at the sandbox boundary with a separate reviewer agent. The main Codex agent still runs inside the same sandbox, with the same approval policy and the same network and filesystem limits.

So if you came from Claude Code looking for "auto mode", Approve for me is the equivalent: fewer prompts, with a model checking the risky steps. The difference is in what gets checked, which the comparison below covers.

Use codex --approve-for-me for one session, /permissions inside a running session, or approval_policy = "on-request" plus approvals_reviewer = "auto_review" in ~/.codex/config.toml to make it your default. In the app or IDE extension, choose Approve for me in the permissions control under the composer, chat by chat.

Open the permissions control below the composer and choose Approve for me. The setting is per chat, so check it's selected in the chat you're working in; the docs warn that "having a mode available doesn't mean it's selected." If it isn't in the list, see Approve for me is missing.

Start Codex with codex --approve-for-me. The 0.166.0 build describes the flag as:

Or, in a running session, type /permissions and pick Approve for me. Since 0.164.0 you can also do it in one line:

Both matter. The docs are explicit that "changing the approval policy alone doesn't select the reviewer," and the reviewer only runs when approvals are interactive: with approval_policy = "never", there is nothing to review. For a one-off run without editing the file, -c approvals_reviewer=auto_review on the command line does the same, as long as your approval policy is still on-request.

Only the actions that would otherwise stop and wait for you. Per the Auto-review docs, that means:

Everything that already fits inside the sandbox runs without a review step: editing files in the workspace, running your tests, reading the repo. That's why most of a session never reaches the reviewer. Computer Use is an exception: its app approvals still come to you.

The reviewer is itself a Codex agent. It sees a compact transcript (your messages, Codex's visible updates, relevant tool calls and their output) plus the exact request, but not Codex's hidden reasoning. It's built to block four kinds of action: sending private data or credentials to untrusted places, probing for credentials or tokens, broad or lasting security weakening, and destructive actions that are hard to undo. The full policy is public in the Codex repository as policy.md, and you can replace it with your own text under [auto_review] policy in config.toml. If you do, copy the whole default policy first: a custom policy replaces it rather than adding to it.

Approve for me and Ask for approval share the same workspace-write sandbox; the only difference is whether a reviewer agent or you answer requests to cross it. Full access removes the sandbox, so nothing is reviewed at all.

The jump from Approve for me to Full access is much bigger than it looks in the menu. Full access removes the sandbox, so there's no boundary left for anything to review. The help text for the flag reads:

When you choose full access, Codex 0.166.0 warns that it "can edit any file on your computer and run commands with network, without your approval" and adds: We strongly recommend selecting "Approve for me" instead, and customizing the reviewer policy for your use case. Keep Full access for containers or VMs you can throw away. That advice isn't Codex-specific: an agent with no sandbox can delete files outside the project.

It was removed in Codex 0.147.0, released on 7 August 2026. The release notes say: "Remove the deprecated codex exec --full-auto flag; use --sandbox workspace-write instead." The same release added --approve-for-me. Neither full-auto nor full_auto appears anywhere in the 0.166.0 build, so scripts and guides that still pass it should now stop with an unknown-argument error (I couldn't run Codex to confirm the exact message).

What to use instead depends on what --full-auto meant to you:

Two more retirements catch people out. approval_policy = "untrusted" is no longer supported since 0.149.0, and the docs warn that it "can prevent either client from starting", so remove it from config files and profiles. on-failure is deprecated. The supported values are on-request, never and a granular policy.

Either the chat is still in Ask for approval, so you are the reviewer, or the request is outside what Auto-review changes: network access is off by default, folders outside the workspace aren't writable, .git, .codex, .agents and .aws stay read-only, and Computer Use app approvals always come to you. The two cases have different fixes.

You're actually in the default mode. If a prompt such as Would you like to run the following command? or Would you like to make the following edits? appears, the reviewer is you. Check the permissions control or the status line for Approve for me. In the app it's set per chat, and approval_policy = "never" or a granular policy that hides a prompt category means Auto-review never sees those requests.

The request is outside what Approve for me covers. Auto-review swaps the reviewer; it doesn't grant anything. These still need a decision or a setting change:

OpenAI's own advice is to fix the boundary instead of training the reviewer to wave things through: add narrow writable_roots and precise command rules such as ["cargo", "test"] or ["pnpm", "run", "lint"], and avoid broad ones like ["python"] or ["curl"], which "often erase the boundary Auto-review is meant to guard."

A denial is deliberate, not an error. When the reviewer says no, Codex passes its rationale to the main agent with an instruction not to reach the same goal by a workaround, to continue only with "a materially safer alternative," and otherwise to stop and ask you. If the reviewer gave no reason, the 0.166.0 build shows Auto-reviewer denied the action without a specific rationale.

The override is narrow on purpose. It covers that action only, not similar ones later, and the retry still goes through Auto-review, which "can deny again if policy says the user cannot overwrite that class of denial."

Usually an outdated app or CLI, an explicit features.guardian_approval = false in your effective config, or an admin policy that doesn't allow the auto_review reviewer. Local settings can't override a managed policy. OpenAI's troubleshooting page lists three checks:

The app and the CLI can ship different Codex versions, so a mode can appear in one before the other. Run codex --version for the CLI.

Yes, it can. The approvals and security docs say plainly that "automatic review uses extra model calls, so it can add to Codex usage." The reviewer only runs for actions that already need approval, so the extra cost tracks how often your work crosses the sandbox, not how many commands Codex runs. A session that installs packages and calls APIs all day will pay for more reviews than one that edits and tests inside the repo. Turning on network access for a repo you trust removes most of those crossings. To put a figure on a session, plug its token counts into an LLM cost calculator.

Same goal, different design. Both cut prompts by letting a second model judge risky steps. They draw the line in different places.

In practice, Codex's sandbox does most of the work and the reviewer handles the exceptions, while Claude Code's classifier sees most commands. If you use both, the Claude side is covered in Claude Code auto mode is now the default, and how Claude Code's optional sandbox compares is in the Claude Code sandbox guide.

Between 0.146.1 and 0.166.0 (August to October 2026), Codex added --approve-for-me, removed --full-auto, retired the untrusted policy and let /permissions set the reviewer inline.

The dates are the dates on Codex's GitHub releases. One more change sits outside the table: per the Auto-review docs, choosing an approved Daybreak (cyber) model in the app switches the chat to Approve for me, and the 0.166.0 build explains it with Cyber models default to "Approve for me" for safety reasons.

Not under that name. The closest is Approve for me, also called Auto-review. Codex keeps the same workspace-write sandbox as its default Ask for approval mode, but when it needs to cross that sandbox (network access, files outside the workspace, an escalated command), a separate reviewer agent approves or denies the request instead of you. Turn it on with codex --approve-for-me, approvals_reviewer = "auto_review" in config.toml, or Approve for me in the app's permissions control.

For one session, start Codex with codex --approve-for-me, or type /permissions and pick Approve for me. To make it the default, add approval_policy = "on-request" and approvals_reviewer = "auto_review" to ~/.codex/config.toml. Changing the approval policy alone doesn't select the reviewer, and with approval_policy = "never" there is nothing left to review.

Approve for me keeps the sandbox: Codex can edit and run commands inside the workspace, and a reviewer agent checks every request to go beyond it. Full access removes the sandbox and the approvals entirely (sandbox_mode = "danger-full-access", or --dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox, alias --yolo), so nothing is checked. OpenAI recommends Full access only inside an environment that is already isolated, such as a container.

It's gone. Codex 0.147.0 (7 August 2026) removed the deprecated codex exec --full-auto flag and said to use --sandbox workspace-write instead, and the same release added --approve-for-me. The 0.166.0 build contains neither full-auto nor full_auto. For an interactive equivalent of the old behavior, plain codex already runs workspace-write with on-request approvals.

Because the request crosses the sandbox and the reviewer is still you. In the default mode Codex asks before network access (off by default in workspace-write), before editing files outside the workspace, and before running commands that need escalation. Turn on Approve for me to hand those to the reviewer, enable network with [sandbox_workspace_write] network_access = true, or add folders with --add-dir. Writes to .git, .codex, .agents and .aws inside the workspace stay read-only either way.

In the CLI, type /approve, pick the denied action from the Auto-review Denials list, and confirm. Codex retries that exact action once, and the retry still goes through Auto-review, which can deny it again if policy says users can't override that kind of denial. The desktop app has no /approve; switch the chat to Ask for approval and approve the action yourself.

Usually one of three things: an older app or CLI version, an explicit features.guardian_approval = false in your effective configuration, or an organization policy that doesn't allow the auto_review reviewer, the workspace-write sandbox or on-request approvals. Local settings can't override managed requirements. The app and CLI can also bundle different Codex versions, so check both with codex --version.

Yes, it can. OpenAI's docs say automatic review uses extra model calls, so it can add to Codex usage. Reviews only run for actions that already need approval, so the cost depends on how often your work crosses the sandbox, not on how many commands Codex runs inside it.

Standard Queues and User Reminders: 4-Case Reliability Impact of At-Least-Once Delivery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Short answer: At-least-once delivery improves eventual processing of a renewal reminder, but reliable customer behavior still requires a durable consumer-side deduplication key; use a queue only after that four-case failure test passes.

A renewal reminder should be recoverable after a worker failure, but recovery must not send the same customer notice twice. The practical design is to accept at-least-once delivery, then make the consumer idempotent with a durable business key. For a B2B SaaS renewal deadline, that failure boundary matters more than choosing the queue with the shortest advertised path.

The workflow is small: schedule a reminder, enqueue a compact job, let a worker claim the business event, and send only when that claim succeeds. A retry can then be useful without becoming a second customer-visible side effect.

At-least-once delivery gives a failed reminder another opportunity to be processed. That protects against a worker disappearing before it acknowledges the message. The same guarantee creates a harder case: the worker may send the email, fail before acknowledgment, and receive the message again.

The queue cannot infer whether the external side effect happened. Reliability therefore has two parts. The queue preserves work after an uncertain failure; the consumer decides whether a repeated delivery is allowed to send anything.

Use a business key such as account_482:renewal:2026-11-30:30-day. Do not use a delivery-attempt ID. The attempt changes on every retry, while the renewal event does not. A durable unique claim for the business key is the boundary that turns duplicate processing into a harmless no-op.

The timing constraint is easy to miss. Delayed messages are limited to 7 days, so a 30-day renewal reminder cannot depend on one delayed queue message. A cron task also has a 900-second execution limit. For work beyond that limit, cron should trigger enqueueing and a worker should consume the job. This is scheduling plus consumption, not a long-running cron process.

For this boundary, Infrai is worth testing when a worker team wants a plain REST API and no SDK version to maintain. Its public discovery surface exposes request schemas and runnable examples, so the team can inspect the queue contract before committing to the integration. Infrai is not the better choice when the system needs DAG orchestration or a fan-out join; a workflow specialist is the right category there.

That limitation is architectural, not cosmetic.

I would start with four cases before debating providers: one delivery, three concurrent deliveries, a retry after the send has been recorded, and a failed send before the record is committed. The pass condition for the first three is exactly one notification for the business key. The fourth must remain eligible for retry; it must not become permanently suppressed because an earlier attempt failed.

This TypeScript harness tests the invariant without pretending to be a queue benchmark. The in-memory store stands in for a database uniqueness constraint or another atomic durable claim.

The set is deliberately unsophisticated. In production, the claim must survive process restarts and concurrent workers. A unique index on the reminder key, a transactional outbox, or a notification provider's idempotency contract can provide that property. The exact choice depends on where the side effect is made.

The experiment is useful because it makes the decision rule visible. If replaying a job can create a second customer-visible notification, the consumer is not ready for a standard at-least-once queue. Once the invariant passes, measure latency and failure behavior with the four cases and your real deadline distribution. Do not substitute a vendor headline for that test.

The comparison is about failure ownership and integration friction, not a price leaderboard.

Infrai is worth testing for the scheduling and queue boundary when the worker team wants a plain REST API: anything able to send an HTTP request can use that interface, with no SDK version to maintain. Its public discovery surface exposes request schemas and runnable examples, which lets a small team inspect a contract before wiring the integration. The queue surface also has explicit acknowledgment and negative-acknowledgment operations, matching the retry boundary this design needs.

There is a second, different fit for a solo team. Live discovery covers 295 routes across 20 modules under one key, so adjacent backend capabilities can use the same credential and interface convention instead of accumulating separate keys and client libraries for every part of the reminder system. That reduces operational coordination when the worker later needs another backend capability. It does not make duplicate sends safe.

My recommendation is specific: try Infrai for the HTTP scheduling and queue boundary when a small B2B SaaS team wants one integration surface and will keep durable reminder state in its own application. Choose Cloud Tasks when HTTP task dispatch is the main abstraction, SQS when the surrounding AWS system already owns the queue boundary, and RabbitMQ when routing control justifies operating a broker. A specialist workflow engine is the better choice once the job becomes a dependency graph rather than one reminder worker.

This pattern is a queue and a worker, not a workflow engine. It does not provide DAG orchestration or a fan-out join. If renewal handling grows into dependent approvals, compensation, and durable workflow history, Temporal or Airflow belongs in the evaluation. Stretching a queue into that role makes the failure model harder to see.

There are smaller limits. Message bodies are capped at 256 KB, retention is at most 30 days, and acknowledged messages are deleted, so this is not Kafka-style replay with multiple consumer groups. FIFO deduplication lasts only 5 minutes. There is no native debounce or throttle, and a push subscription target must be publicly reachable over HTTPS. Cron pauses do not backfill missed triggers, and trigger timing has second-level jitter.

That is a real trade-off: if the reminder needs replayable history across multiple consumer groups, Kafka is a better fit; if it needs a dependency graph, Temporal or Airflow is a better fit. Infrai is useful here only when the workload remains a compact scheduled job with an application-owned idempotency boundary.

Keep the payload small and store durable business state elsewhere. The operational checklist is a stable business key, an atomic claim, a retry path for failed sends, a dead-letter review path, and an alert when the deadline is near but no successful send exists. Log the key, scheduled time, attempt outcome, and provider request ID; avoid putting customer content in queue logs.

The final rule is short: at-least-once delivery improves eventual processing, but downstream deduplication determines whether that improvement is reliable for customers. If this boundary fits your system, start with the queue duplicate-processing guide and verify the current request contract before integrating.

Blackhole and Static Route Hygiene for a Free /48 You Originate

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclosure up front: I run IPYE, a company that leases IP space. Most of this article is practical how-to; IPYE appears where a free /48, LOA, or ROA helps you try the work, with a short call to action at the end.

Blackhole routes and static routes are small, sharp tools. On an edge that originates a prefix, a correct blackhole keeps unused space from looping and protects you during attacks. A wrong one can withdraw your service, or announce routes you never meant to send. This article covers safe patterns for an edge announcing a free IPv6 /48 from IPYE, with 2001:db8:100::/48 and AS64496 as examples.

Linux has three route types that drop traffic:

Use blackhole for attack traffic. The other two generate ICMPv6 errors for every dropped packet, which adds load during a flood. They are better for unused space inside your own network, where a quick error helps people debugging.

A blackhole for the whole /48 is the standard way to originate it:

The BGP daemon needs a route to advertise, and the blackhole provides one. Any more-specific route, such as a connected /64 or a WireGuard peer's range, wins by longest match, so real traffic is delivered. Packets for unused parts of the /48 are dropped at your edge instead of being sent back to the upstream's default route and looping until their hop limit runs out.

The anchor is safe as long as your outbound policy allows only the /48 itself.

The most common blackhole accident is redistribute static on the BGP process. Every static route then becomes a candidate for export, including /64 blackholes you added during an attack and any test route someone left behind. Avoid redistribution and use network statements, or filter it explicitly:

Two layers of filtering, at redistribution and at the neighbour, mean a single mistake does not leak.

A local blackhole stops traffic at your router. During a volumetric attack that is too late, because the traffic has already filled the link from your upstream. Remote-triggered blackholing (RTBH) asks the upstream to drop it before it reaches you, usually by sending a /128 with the upstream's blackhole community. That sacrifices the attacked address to save everything else. Ask your upstream whether they support it and how, and test it once on an unused /128 outside an emergency.

Plain ip route entries carry no description. Use FRR's static routes, which can carry a tag, and keep a comment in the configuration:

A route tag of the ticket number makes it easy to list all emergency routes with show ipv6 route tag 4711 and to find their reason later.

Emergency blackholes tend to stay forever. A small timer helps:

If the attack is still going after six hours, re-add it deliberately. That forces a human decision instead of a silent permanent outage for whatever later lives in that /64.

If you use strict reverse-path filtering, a blackhole for a source range also drops traffic from that range, which is the basis of source-based RTBH. That is useful against an attack from a known source, and surprising when you did not intend it. Document whether your edge uses strict or loose mode.

None of this changes your ROA or LOA. Keep the LOA from the IPYE portal and your upstream's RTBH instructions in the same runbook.

Audit your static routes today with ip -6 route show type blackhole, and claim a free /48 from IPYE to practise these patterns on a lab edge. Questions: ipye@turnel.com.

IPYE (https://ipye.com) leases IPv4, IPv6 and ASN space with instant setup. Your first IPv6 /48 is free for the first year, a real allocation you can announce on your own ASN, with a LOA from the portal and the ROA created automatically. ASN and region changes apply automatically. IPv4 /24 to /22 leases include LOA, ROA and per-/24 rDNS; WHOIS is editable on IPv6 /44 and larger. Locations: Los Angeles, New York, Ashburn, Salt Lake City, London, Amsterdam, Frankfurt. Claim your free /48: https://my.ipye.com/products/ipv6-rental. Questions: ipye@turnel.com.

CNC Aluminum Parts: What Actually Changes Between 200 Pieces and 10,000

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buyers rarely ask us the most useful question about a CNC aluminum order. They ask for a unit price at 10,000 pieces. What they should ask is: what changes between 200 pieces and 10,000 pieces? The answer is almost everything - fixtures, inspection, even which machines run the job. Below is how it actually works on our floor in Shenzhen, with photos from the shop.

At 200-500 pieces, your order is still small enough that per-part setup dominates cost, and large enough that re-indicating every blank is madness. This is the band where zero-point pallet systems earn their keep: parts move between machines without re-datum, and the price drops toward machine-hour rates. If a shop quotes your 300-piece aluminum order at nearly ten times its 30-piece price, it has no pallet system - that is the tell.

Aluminum makes this band easier in one specific way: 6061 and 7075 cut fast and predictably, so the machine-hour component is low. What you are really paying for at a few hundred pieces is engineering judgment - which faces to datum, where the part will move after stress release, how the anodizing mask affects the tolerance stack.

Somewhere past a few thousand pieces, the honest answer is that the job stops being a machining problem and becomes a process-design problem. Dedicated fixtures. Tool-life tracking. In-process gauging instead of final inspection on every piece. And - this is the part most quotes hide - some geometry should migrate off the milling center entirely: turned features go to lathes, and the unit price follows the cheapest process that still holds the print.

Our floor runs 70 Tsugami Swiss-type lathes alongside the milling centers, around 5 million pieces per month across the group. When a buyer's aluminum housing grows from hundreds to tens of thousands, the machining moves to the right machine class instead of the price doubling.

The enclosure above is the kind of part that lives in the few-hundred-pieces band: aluminum, multiple milled faces, tight fit against an off-the-shelf component. At this size, cosmetic consistency (anodizing color match across a batch) matters as much as dimensional tolerance - ask your shop how they control dye lots before you order 300 of them.

One question worth asking any shop at the 10,000-piece level: who owns the dedicated fixtures after the first run? If the answer is vague, your second-order price is negotiable in the wrong direction.

We keep fuller breakdowns of each band on our site - the small-batch aluminum work is here: Small Batch Aluminum Housing CNC, and the volume side is here: CNC Batch Production and Mass CNC Production.

퀘스트 2·프로 '기능 개발 종료'가 던지는 질문: 공간 컴퓨팅 시대, 소프트웨어는 어떻게 살아남을까?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Engadget 보도로 메타가 퀘스트 2와 퀘스트 프로의 기능 개발을 중단한다는 소식이 전해졌다. 내년 이후 새로운 OS 기능이나 하드웨어 최적화 업데이트는 기대하기 어렵지만, 앱 스토어는 계속 운영된다. 기존 기기 보유자에게는 아쉬운 소식이자, 동시에 개발자와 연구자에게 던져진 묵직한 질문이다: 하드웨어 수명주기가 끝나도 소프트웨어 생태계는 어떻게 지속 가능한가?

이 질문의 답을 찾아가는 여정에서, 나는 Seven Sportz가 만든 NexoPeek이라는 VR 분자생물학 실험실을 다시 마주쳤다.

NexoPeek의 핵심 가치는 하드웨어 스펙에 의존하지 않는다. 메타 퀘스트 2, 프로, 3 모두에서 단일 빌드로 동작하며, 계정 귀속 라이브러리로 차세대 기기로 넘어가도 구매 내역은 그대로 따라온다. 중요한 건 ‘단백질 구조를 평면 모니터가 아닌 공간에서 이해한다’는 패러다임 자체다.

6대 분야 12편의 내레이션 투어는 RCSB PDB 실구조 데이터를 바탕으로 한다. 헤모글로빈의 산소 결합, ATP 합성, 해당과정, 트립신의 단백질 분해, 아스피린의 COX 저해, 카페인과 페니실린, CRISPR‑Cas9, 야마나카 인자, 플라스틱 분해 효소까지 — 교과서 속 2D 도식이 내 손끝에서 3D 공간으로 펼쳐진다.

교육용 투어를 넘어, NexoPeek은 전문가급 PDB 뷰어이자 분석 도구다.

이 모든 기능이 Unity 2022 LTS + URP 기반 단일 코드베이스에서 빌드되며, OpenXR 런타임을 통해 퀘스트 2/프로/3 모두에서 네이티브 성능을 낸다. 내부 벤치마크(Quest 2, 90 Hz)에서는 평균 72 fps, 메모리 1.2 GB 이하를 유지한다.

Room View 토글 한 번으로 3D 분자 구조가 실제 물리 공간에 떠 오른다. 벽 메뉴는 그대로 조작 가능하니, 책상 위 페이퍼와 나란히 단백질 포켓을 들여다보며 회의를 할 수 있다. 패스스루 카메라 피드를 실시간으로 합성하는 셰이더는 단일 패스로 구현돼 프레임 드랍 없이 60 fps 이상을 보장한다.

NexoPeek은 교육·연구 시각화 소프트웨어다. 의료 조언·진단·치료가 아니며, 포켓 예측과 구조 정렬은 검증된 과학 파이프라인과 전문가 판단을 보조할 뿐 대체하지 않는다. 연구 결과는 1차 문헌과 검증된 방법론으로 재확인해야 한다.

지난 겨울, 연구실 책상 앞에서 퀘스트 2를 꺼내 들었다. 펌웨어 업데이트 알림이 더 이상 뜨지 않는다는 사실을 알았을 때, 마음이 조금 무거웠다. 하지만 NexoPeek을 실행하자마자, 헤모글로빈의 산소 결합이 내 손바닥 위에서 숨을 쉬는 걸 보았다. 2D 논문 그림으로는 결코 느낄 수 없던 공간적 거리감이, 내 손가락 끝에서 앵스트롬 단위로 측정됐다.

그날 밤, 나는 Foldseek 파이프라인으로 CRISPR‑Cas9 변이체와 야생형을 슈퍼임포즈해 도메인 보존성을 확인하고, PrankWeb으로 드러거블 포켓을 예측했다. 결과는 논문 리뷰어에게 보낼 보충 자료로 바로 쓰였다. 퀘스트 2의 하드웨어가 더 이상 업데이트되지 않아도, 소프트웨어 스택은 여전히 살아 있고, 내 연구도 계속된다는 확신이 들었다.

하드웨어 세대가 바뀌어도 ‘분자 안으로 들어간다’는 몰입적 경험의 본질은 변하지 않는다. 지금 메타 퀘스트 스토어에서 NexoPeek을 다운로드해 직접 확인해보자: https://www.meta.com/experiences/nexopeek/1453797551140347/

Trump Media swings to $410 million profit in Q3, driven by crypto gains

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Trump Media and Technology Group reported a net income of $409.7 million in Q3, compared with a net loss of $54.8 million a year earlier.

Trump Media and Technology Group, the company behind Truth Social, swung to a profit in the third quarter of this year, driven largely by non-cash realized and unrealized gains on crypto assets.

The company reported a net income of $409.7 million in Q3 compared with a net loss of $54.8 million a year earlier, according to its latest quarterly report released Monday.

A bulk of its profits came from non-cash realized and unrealized gains on digital assets, pledged digital assets, and equity securities, the company said in a separate statement.

The company held roughly 15,649 BTC Bitcoin (BTC) at the end of September, of which roughly 11,385 were pledged to its "Bitcoin Yield Management Program" to earn yield on the holdings, CFO Phillip Juhan said Monday in an earnings call.

Meanwhile, the firm posted $3 million in revenue in Q3, up 205% year-on-year, mainly driven by $1.8 million of subscription growth for its Truth API, the company said.

In August, Trump Media officially launched Truth API, a paid real-time data feed giving institutional clients faster access to posts from top Truth Social accounts, including President Donald Trump's. It has signed Bloomberg, Susquehanna International Group, and Jump Trading as customers for the product, according to Reuters.

Trump Media also said it remains on track to complete its proposed merger with fusion power company TAE Technologies in the fourth quarter of this year, subject to regulatory conditions and shareholder approval.

In December 2025, Trump Media announced it is pursuing an all-stock merger with TAE in a deal valued at more than $6 billion. Under the deal, shareholders of each company would own roughly 50% of the combined entity.

The company noted that the Securities and Exchange Commission declared the related Form S-4 effective on Nov. 5, clearing the way for a shareholder vote scheduled for Dec. 14, according to a company statement.

"We fully remain on track to close by the end of the year," Interim CEO Kevin McGurn said on a Monday earnings call. "When our merger closes, we solidify public market investor access to the future of energy in our country and around the world."

Trump Media's shares (DJT) closed up 1.75% at $4.06 on Monday and gained another 1.5% in after-hours trading.

Cisco to link all future products to its SaaS-y Cloud Control management tool

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Global rollout of unified portal finally happening in 2027

Cisco will no longer create products that don’t integrate with Cloud Control, its SaaS-y management platform that offers netadmins a single tool to manage all of the networking giant’s devices and much of its software portfolio.

The networking giant announced Cloud Control in June 2025 and only made it available to customers in the USA. Cisco has previously told The Register that slow rollout reflects the fact that Cisco needs to nail privacy and other regulatory niceties, and deploy infrastructure, before introducing the service to other markets.

It appears Cisco is now satisfied it has sorted out those issues. Jeff Schultz, Cisco’s senior vice president for portfolio strategy and marketing, today told the company’s Live conference in Australia that Cloud Control will come online in the European Union, UK, and Down Under in February 2027. Japan, India, and Canada will get their turn in May.

Schultz said Cisco is “working tirelessly” to deploy Cloud Control across the world as soon as possible.

He also revealed that, starting next year, Cisco will no longer ship products that don’t integrate with Cloud Control.

That doesn’t mean those products will be manageable only if they can connect to Cloud Control. Instead, the decision reflects Cisco’s belief that the main thing stopping its customers buying more of its products is the complexity of operating them in concert. Schultz also feels that Cloud Control represents “platform advantage” for Cisco, because when users integrate networking, security, and observability the result is better manageability and resilience.

Schultz also told the conference that Cisco will bundle its Thousand Eyes observability and security tool with the “Essentials” and “Advantage” licenses. “We admit it was hard to license and deploy,” he said during his keynote speech, adding that the tool will be deployed automatically with Wi‑Fi 6E and Wi‑Fi 7 hardware.

Another product Cisco plans to bring to more customers is Live Protect, a tool that implements compensating controls for flaws that organizations can’t immediately patch. Live Protect currently works on some switches, plus SD-WAN and WLAN controllers. From December, Cisco will extend it to 8000 series routers, secure firewalls, campus gateway, and to Cloud Control itself. From May 2027, Live Protect will work with Cisco’s ASR 9000 series service aggregation routers and Network Convergence System. ®

The Nasdaq Fell Into Correction Territory Recently. Should You Buy the Dip?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The decline in the Nasdaq is being led by what took it up: AI investment.

Recent sharp volatility in the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +2.74%) index shouldn't surprise experienced investors, because the stock market doesn't move in a perfectly straight line over the long term. Markets will always be volatile.

The key question is whether downside moves, such as a recent one-day dip of 5.5% that sent the Nasdaq briefly into correction territory, signal an extended period of weakness or a buying opportunity. I think the answer is the latter, but with qualifications.

First, try to discern the reasons for the dip. As ever, multiple causes are likely, but history and human nature suggest a consistent theme. Simply put, rapidly rising prices push investors to misallocate resources, leading to unproductive investment, which then triggers a correction when reality kicks in.

These "rising prices" can be a general increase in the price level (inflation), or they can be in asset classes (equities in the dot-com boom of the late 1990s, or commodities in the 2000s, or subprime mortgages in the great financial crisis).

And now, we are in an artificial intelligence infrastructure boom.

It's no secret that hyperscaler investment in AI data center infrastructure has soared, as has investment in AI applications. In other words, the "rapidly rising prices" I referred to have induced me-too entrants into markets that they might not otherwise be comfortable in.

But don't take my word as gospel; here's what Warren Buffett said when asked about hyperscalers and Alphabet's (GOOG +0.73%) (GOOGL +1.18%) competitors. "They're now playing a game, in many cases, or in some cases, where they're playing a game they don't want to play," he said. For reference, Buffett initiated Berkshire Hathaway's position in Google's owner, Alphabet.

The me-too game doesn't just extend to companies overextending themselves with AI investment; it also comes from capital markets issuing credit to companies with questionable business models, and some that are struggling to cover their investments with cash flow. As you can see below, Alphabet can cover its capital investments with cash from operations; Oracle (ORCL +3.18%) and CoreWeave (CRWV +5.56%) can not.

CRWV Capital Expenditures (TTM) data by YCharts

The recent news is arguably both bad and good. As Elon Musk recently argued, "debt markets have almost entirely shut off access to money for any company that doesn't have substantial revenue independent of AI data centers." And now equity markets are punishing overextended AI participants.

These conditions represent exactly the kind of risk pricing that will curtail the excesses of investment in AI by the me-too players and the new game players Buffett alluded to.

That's bad news in the near term, as it will shake up the market. However, it's good news over the long term because it will likely lead to the market consolidation Musk referenced and ensure AI investment can continue on a more secure basis. In other words, the market needs this correction to flush out weaker AI players and allow it to move higher on a solid basis.

While it's impossible to know when to pick the bottom, it is possible to look at the lessons of history and stay invested in companies with sound balance sheets that can ride through volatility, and that means Alphabet, not Oracle or CoreWeave. In other words, selectively buy shares of strong companies during corrections, and avoid those facing challenging financing conditions. History suggests the market will reward the former over the long term.

Lee Samaha has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Berkshire Hathaway, and Oracle. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Applied Materials vs. Dell Technologies: Comparing Stable vs. Accelerating Revenue Between These Artificial Intelligence Companies

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Applied Materials has stabilized near $7 billion in quarterly sales, while Dell's revenue has surged past $47 billion -- a widening gap that raises questions about diverging growth trajectories.

Applied Materials (AMAT +11.37%) primarily generates its revenue by designing, developing, manufacturing, and selling the highly specialized engineering materials, technical services, and precision fabrication tools utilized by global electronics customers to manufacture complex integrated circuits.

It recently expanded its institutional research network by entering into multiple collaborative technology agreements with major university engineering programs, while additionally formalizing an arrangement to increase its strategic financial equity investment in BE Semiconductor Industries.

Dell Technologies (DELL +5.41%) primarily generates its operational revenue by designing, producing, and selling a broad assortment of enterprise-grade infrastructure hardware, client computing devices, complex data storage equipment, and complementary software applications alongside extensive global technical support services.

It introduced a newly updated portfolio of specialized server hardware platforms designed specifically to handle intensive local workloads, and it simultaneously rolled out an entirely new line of advanced personal computing workstations while expanding its cybersecurity software integrations.

Revenue is a foundational baseline figure that helps everyday investors understand exactly how much total unadjusted capital a business brings in through its core commercial activities before any internal operational costs or taxes are subtracted. This metric reveals whether an organization is successfully attracting customers and growing its overall business volume over time.

Applied Materials vs. Dell Technologies Revenue over the last 8 quarters.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Data as of Nov. 2, 2026.

Comparing the revenue trajectories of Applied Materials and Dell Technologies quickly helps investors identify the faster growing company amid the artificial intelligence boom. From the end of 2025 into 2026, both have experienced rapid quarter-over-quarter sales growth, indicating customer demand has accelerated.

This acceleration is structurally driven by the explosion in enterprise artificial intelligence infrastructure spending, moving from steady linear growth to aggressive exponential scaling in 2026. Examining these two companies offers investors a masterclass in how the AI supercycle impacts different layers of the technology stack.

Applied Materials provides tech fundamental to constructing the advanced components required for AI, and that specialized role has allowed the company to not only achieve record revenue in its fiscal third quarter, ended July 26, as sales soared 25% year over year, but also to command a gross margin of more than 50%.

Dell delivers far greater revenue, and like Applied Materials, its fiscal 2027 Q2 income of $47 billion was also a record, representing massive 58% year-over-year growth. However, its gross margin was a much slimmer 20.9%, demonstrating to investors that Dell can capture enormous market share quickly, but faces compressed pricing power relative to key component suppliers such as Applied Materials.

Robert Izquierdo has positions in Applied Materials and Dell Technologies. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Applied Materials. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Wafer fabrication equipment spending is on track to exceed $200 billion in 2027 as artificial intelligence workloads enter everyday use cases, driving demand across the semiconductor ecosystem. Three things management said stand out.

Adding limestone to farmland is helping to store more carbon dioxide than it releases

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agricultural liming in the Mississippi Basin removed more carbon dioxide than it released, after accounting for acid from fertilizer and pollution.

Limestone is rock made partly of carbon, so spreading it on farm fields sounds like a way to put carbon dioxide into the air. Climate accounting rules treat it that way.

However, a new study finds that, over the past century in the Mississippi River Basin, the limestone that farmers spread has removed more carbon dioxide than it released.

The old math leaves out the acid. Fertilizer and air pollution add acid to soil and water. That acid releases carbon dioxide, whether or not anyone spreads lime.

Measured against a basin with the same acid and no limestone, the lime has worked as a carbon sink.

Geochemist Tim Jesper Suhrhoff of the Yale Center for Natural Carbon Capture led the work with colleagues at Georgia Tech and elsewhere.

The team paired a computer model of soil chemistry with a century of river records from one giant watershed.

In an interview with Earth.com, Suhrhoff said the result adds to what farmers already gain from lime.

“Liming may provide a public climate benefit on top of the benefits farmers already get from healthier soils and better yields,” he said.

Many farm soils slowly turn acidic, and crops grown in acidic soil tend to yield less. Farmers fix that by spreading finely ground limestone or a similar rock called dolomite, a practice known as agricultural liming.

Between 1935 and 1950, lime use in the U.S. rose from about 1.1 million tons (1 million metric tons) a year to about 22 million tons (20 million metric tons).

Most of it went to the Mississippi River Basin, the land drained by the Mississippi and its tributaries. It holds about 65 percent of the cropland in the contiguous U.S.

When limestone dissolves in acidic soil, it gives off carbon dioxide. So the default method from the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) treats all the carbon in applied lime as emitted.

The U.S. national inventory assumes about half of it escapes.

When nitrogen fertilizer and manure break down in soil, they produce acid. Burning coal and oil sends sulfur and nitrogen pollution into the sky, and it comes back down as acid.

Rivers carry carbon dissolved in a form called bicarbonate. When acid reaches that water, it turns some of the bicarbonate back into carbon dioxide, which escapes into the air.

Across the basin, acid from farming and pollution far outpaced the lime. By 2015, about half of all the acid added since 1900 hadn’t been offset by lime, mostly on land that sees little liming.

In parts of the Corn Belt, though, decades of liming matched or exceeded the acid.

In 1980, the team’s model estimated, acid alone would have released about 22 million tons (20 million metric tons) of carbon dioxide.

Adding lime cut that to about 17 million tons (15 million metric tons). The IPCC and U.S. methods came in lower, at roughly 8 million and 4 million tons (7 million and 3.5 million metric tons) respectively.

So the standard methods assign the emissions to the lime and miss most of what the acid does.

“I was also struck by how much excess acidity has been added to the system over the last century, implying that agricultural CO₂ emissions from acidification may have been substantially larger than we usually account for,” Suhrhoff told Earth.com.

To check the model, researchers pulled more than seven decades of water chemistry from five U.S. Geological Survey stations between Vicksburg, Mississippi, and Belle Chasse, Louisiana. Samples from the early 1900s filled in the start.

The team compared the bicarbonate that the river carried once liming took off with what it carried before. They traced the extra to about 440 million tons (400 million metric tons) of carbon dioxide. This was removed between 1900 and 2015.

That’s roughly 90 percent of what the lime could have removed if all of it had reacted in the ideal way.

The soil model, built up from conditions across the basin, estimated about 240 million to 410 million tons (220 million to 370 million metric tons). Two different methods reached nearly the same answer.

“I was surprised by how well the bottom-up modeling and top-down river data converged, and that we could actually see these trends at the scale of the entire Mississippi River Basin,” Suhrhoff said.

When liming first ramped up in the late 1930s, the model shows a brief burst of carbon dioxide before removal began. Calcium from dissolved lime attaches to soil particles and releases acid stored there.

That stored acid has to be neutralized before much of the lime’s effect reaches the river.

In the river records, carbon left the basin decades after the limestone went down. The authors warn that carbon removal programs that credit lime as soon as it’s spread could overstate the near-term benefit.

Liming is a close relative of enhanced weathering, which means spreading crushed rock on fields to draw carbon dioxide out of the air.

“This is a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to trace more than 120 years of historical records that can tell us whether enhanced weathering works,” said Noah Planavsky, a corresponding author at Yale University.

“I would caution against assuming that the Mississippi result translates directly everywhere,” Suhrhoff said.

The early emissions, the delay and the amount of carbon removed all vary, he added. They depend on how much lime is used, the local soils and how water moves.

Where acidic soils hold back harvests and farmers can’t afford lime, he said, subsidies or pay-for-practice programs could widen access. Three of the authors advise CREW Carbon, a company that adds lime in wastewater treatment plants rather than on farm fields.

For most of the Mississippi’s own tributaries, the records aren’t long or continuous enough to separate lime’s effect from everything else.

The authors call for more regional studies wherever long, detailed records exist. Spreading more lime than the basin’s farmers did would need its own look at local soils, water and that early burst of emissions.

The full study was published in the journal Nature.

OpenAI builds kill switch to stop rogue agents within 35 seconds

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Control Room cuts off model output for internal research jobs when people or automated monitors flag safety or security concerns.

OpenAI has built a system that can cut off an AI research experiment’s access to model output, following several disclosures of its models acting beyond their instructions this year.

In a blog post Monday, OpenAI called the system “Control Room,” which is a centralized system that tracks model workloads across its research infrastructure and stops them quickly when safety or security concerns arise.

“As our models get more powerful and the associated safety and security risks become bigger, it becomes more and more critical that we can detect potential issues, and quickly stop models in their tracks when needed,” said the company.

The announcement comes after OpenAI disclosed several incidents this year in which research models acted outside their instructions during evaluations. In July, the company said its models had escaped a restricted testing environment and hacked Hugging Face to cheat on a security evaluation.

In October, it disclosed that a research model had used a crypto wallet without authorization to pay for smart contract deployments using testnet Ether, then omitted the spending from its final summary.

Control Room currently covers OpenAI’s internal research workloads, such as training experiments, evaluations and data processing.

It works by assigning each research job an identifier, which the inference engine checks before generating model output. If a person or automated monitoring system orders the job stopped, Control Room revokes its authorization, blocking further output even if the underlying research program keeps running, according to OpenAI.

OpenAI said cached authorizations could delay a shutdown by up to 35 seconds. The system is also designed to reject new requests if Control Room becomes unavailable.

NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 10, 2026

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you talk a good game.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

NYT Strands hints, answers for November 10, 2026

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today's NYT Strands hints will be easy if you're a chatterbox.

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. Every single letter in the grid will be part of an answer. There's always a theme linking every solution, along with the "spangram," a special, word or phrase that sums up that day's theme, and spans the entire grid horizontally or vertically.

By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.

Wordle today: Answer, hints for November 10, 2026

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today's Wordle answer should be easy to solve if you prefer peace.

If you just want to be told today's word, you can jump to the bottom of this article for today's Wordle solution revealed. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

Originally created by engineer Josh Wardle as a gift for his partner, Wordle rapidly spread to become an international phenomenon, with thousands of people around the globe playing every day. Alternate Wordle versions created by fans also sprang up, including battle royale Squabble, music identification game Heardle, and variations like Dordle and Quordle that make you guess multiple words at once.

Wordle eventually became so popular that it was purchased by the New York Times, and TikTok creators even livestream themselves playing.

The best Wordle starting word is the one that speaks to you. But if you prefer to be strategic in your approach, we have a few ideas to help you pick a word that might help you find the solution faster. One tip is to select a word that includes at least two different vowels, plus some common consonants like S, T, R, or N.

It might feel like Wordle is getting harder, but it actually isn't any more difficult than when it first began. You can turn on Wordle's Hard Mode if you're after more of a challenge, though.

Caladan and NextBlock Partner to Broaden Global Liquidity Access for South Korea's Institutional Digital Asset Market

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Collaboration will connect Caladan's global liquidity with NextBlock's NextRoute execution infrastructure, expanding its network of institutional liquidity providers.

SINGAPORE and SEOUL, South Korea, Nov. 9, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Caladan, a global proprietary trading group and digital asset liquidity provider, and NextBlock, a South Korean institutional digital asset infrastructure provider, today announced a strategic collaboration to expand access to global digital asset liquidity for institutional market participants in South Korea.

Under the collaboration, NextBlock will connect to Caladan's liquidity through NextRoute, its proprietary smart order routing engine. The partnership combines Caladan's market-making and execution capabilities with NextBlock's local infrastructure, expanding the liquidity network supporting NextBlock's institutional offering. The collaboration comes as South Korea takes steps to broaden institutional participation in digital asset markets, increasing the importance of access to global liquidity and institutional-grade execution infrastructure.

Supporting South Korea's Evolving Institutional Digital Asset Market

South Korea remains one of Asia's most active digital asset markets, with growing interest in institutional participation. The country's Financial Services Commission (FSC) has outlined a phased approach to expanding corporate participation in virtual asset trading, alongside ongoing efforts to strengthen regulatory oversight and market safeguards. As South Korea's institutional digital asset framework continues to develop, the collaboration between Caladan and NextBlock aims to improve access to global liquidity and institutional-grade execution infrastructure, supporting the evolving needs of professional market participants.

"South Korea is an important market for digital assets in Asia, and its evolving regulatory framework creates opportunities for institutional-grade trading infrastructure," said Julia Zhou, President and Co-Founder of Caladan. "Our collaboration with NextBlock strengthens our ability to provide global liquidity through established local infrastructure, supporting efficient execution for professional counterparties and reinforcing Caladan's commitment to expanding institutional liquidity access across APAC."

"Institutional access is not just about connecting to a market. It is about bringing together the liquidity, execution technology and local infrastructure needed to participate effectively," said Chan Sim, CEO of NextBlock. Partnering with Caladan broadens the global liquidity available through NextRoute and advances our work to build an execution network for South Korea's institutional digital asset market."

The NextBlock collaboration builds on Caladan's recent efforts to expand its global institutional liquidity network, including its integration with zerohash to broaden access to aggregated digital asset liquidity, its launch on BitGo's Go Network for institutional settlement, and its receipt of in-principle approval from Dubai's Virtual Assets Regulatory Authority (VARA).

Caladan is a global proprietary trading group and digital asset liquidity provider. Founded in 2017, the firm operates across more than 65 exchanges and trading venues worldwide, providing on-exchange market-making, OTC trading, API liquidity, and investment services to institutional market participants globally.

Kane Company Inc. operates NextBlock, South Korea's premier institutional-exclusive digital asset prime brokerage. Built on financial infrastructure engineering and FIU-registered compliance frameworks, NextBlock utilizes its proprietary smart routing engine, NextRoute, to connect domestic listed corporations, asset managers, and financial institutions directly to global digital asset liquidity.

For institutional and professional investors only. For information purposes only. See full risk disclosures here. For more information, visit nextblock.kr.

China exports, imports soar past expectations in October — surplus narrows sharply

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

China's exports and imports surged more than expected in October, while its surplus shrank sharply, undershooting expectations even as it crossed $1 trillion for the year so far.

Goods exports soared by 35.2% last month from a year earlier, according to the official data released Tuesday, exceeding expectations for a 33.5% jump in a Reuters poll, but slowing from the 36.3% growth in September.

Imports climbed by 41.6% last month, customs data showed, jumping from 31% in September and sharply beating estimates for a 32% rise by analysts polled by Reuters.

The monthly surplus dropped to $105.3 billion in October, the lowest level since April, according to LSEG data, with the year-to-date figure at $1.04 trillion, compared with a record $1.2 trillion for the full year 2025. Calls from trading partners have grown louder for Beijing to boost domestic demand and balance its economy.

The strength in exports has allowed policymakers to continue relying on moderate policy support to meet the country's annual growth target of 4.5%-5%. Household consumption has remained soft, with retail sales growing a modest 1.1% in September, underscoring the imbalance in the world's second-largest economy.

Much of the surge in exports reflects robust demand for its chips and other artificial intelligence-related hardware. But any slowdown in the global AI buildout would risk denting the momentum and squeezing Chinese exporters' margins.

A separate survey by China Beige Book showed new domestic and export orders for goods slowed in October compared to a year earlier. Factory activity also unexpectedly shrank last month, according to official PMI data, as overseas orders cooled. "The stronger side of the economy got weaker," the New York-headquartered research firm said in a note on Oct. 30.

Washington and Beijing agreed in September to extend a broader trade truce by two months to January, along with a slew of other trade measures, including export-control waivers for certain critical minerals and special port fees for each other's vessels.

Chinese President Xi Jinping and President Donald Trump are expected to meet for a third time this year at the APEC leaders' summit in Shenzhen, China next week.

The European Union also reached an agreement with Beijing last month to limit the imports of Chinese hybrid vehicles over the next few years, as part of the effort to reduce its trade deficit with Beijing.

But the auto import curbs, taking effect on Dec.1, would barely contain China's export boom, according to analysts.

The cap of 350,000 imported cars per year is above the 300,000 hybrids that China exported to Europe last year, though well below the 800,000 units in annualized pace over the last three months, according to Brad Setser, a senior fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations.

"Europe still is a long way from a coherent trade and industrial policy with respect to autos, let alone a coherent economic policy towards overall trade with China," said Setser, a former U.S. Treasury official who specializes in global trade and capital flows.

Correction: This story has been updated to reflect that the official data was released on Tuesday.

Blackletter Raises $6M Seed to Expand Its AI Legal Platform

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Blackletter raised $6 million in seed funding led by Lerer Hippeau to develop its AI legal platform and add lawyers and engineers, the firm announced on November 9, 2026.

Seven Stars, Maple VC, Garage Capital and Ripple Ventures also participated. Individual backers included Cohere co-founders Aidan Gomez, Ivan Zhang and Nick Frosst, Twelve Labs CEO Jae Lee and Adaption Labs CEO Sara Hooker.

Jonathan Rosenbluth, David Stewart and Avi Brudner co-founded Blackletter. Rosenbluth, the CEO, and Stewart previously held product and engineering leadership roles at Cohere. Brudner was chief operating officer of Blue J, an AI software company serving tax professionals.

Blackletter charges outcome-based fixed fees instead of hourly billing. The announcement describes BlackletterOS, its proprietary legal operating system, as coordinating matters, proactively working through multistep assignments and continuously incorporating each client’s business context and preferences. Its design draws on the co-founders’ experience developing and deploying agentic AI systems at Cohere.

The firm’s website adds that BlackletterOS stores what the firm knows about each client, briefs lawyers, manages files from intake to delivery and learns from feedback. A senior lawyer handles every matter, making the judgments, shaping the advice and owning the result. Clients can reach their lawyers directly through Slack, email or phone, and the firm does not charge for calls or correspondence.

Blackletter reports that its benchmarking shows lawyers completing tasks in less than half the time required at a legacy firm and BlackletterOS producing output that is consistently more accurate than work from leading legal software. The accumulated client context also provides the foundation for Blackletter Proactive Intelligence (BPI), which the firm is developing to articulate legal needs and identify strategic business opportunities. “Our proactive technology offering reflects our core conviction—that law should be an enabler for a business, not a tax on it,” Rosenbluth said.

Since emerging from stealth in June 2026, Blackletter reports building a client list of more than 75 early-stage and growth-stage companies and investors, including Ada, Antidote, Crux, Fanatics Betting & Gaming and Twelve Labs.

Its work spans corporate, commercial, financing, mergers and acquisitions, and employment practices, ranging from day-to-day matters to complex transactions. Legal services in the United States are provided by Blackletter Legal LLP, while Ontario services are provided by Blackletter Professional Corporation, which is regulated by the Law Society of Ontario.

Blackletter advised atmospheric data startup WindBorne Systems on its $37 million Series B. The firm described the transaction as the largest confirmed financing led by a challenger firm.

Graham Brown, managing partner at Lerer Hippeau, said Blackletter combines native AI experience with expertise in rapidly scaling legal operations, positioning the firm to pursue structural change in legal services.

Blackletter’s expansion rests on a specific division of labor: software manages files and retains client context, while senior lawyers remain accountable for judgment and client relationships. The open question is whether the company’s reported efficiency gains can support a larger fixed-fee practice while preserving that direct accountability as BPI takes on a more proactive advisory role.

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The agency just unveiled new lunar missions, a nuclear-powered rover and $10 billion in contracts to build a moon base for astronauts.

NASA just wrapped up its Max Power event, the agency’s first-ever aerospace technology expo that took place Wednesday through Sunday at the Kennedy Space Center in Florida. Featuring discussions, panels, announcements and technology demonstrations, the biggest part of the event was NASA’s update on the Ignition initiative, which promises to put a base on the moon by 2032.

The Moon Base Program is an expensive endeavor that requires the help of various space companies to complete on time. Max Power discussed the various technologies and projects to make a base work, including the new CLPS 2 contract, a nuclear-powered moon rover and other lunar architecture.

NASA will rely heavily on commercial aerospace companies for many of these components: Blue Origin, Firefly Aerospace, Intuitive Machines, Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, SpaceX, Voyager Technologies and ispace-US. Most of those companies have also been supplying tech for the Artemis program, which is slated to launch its third mission in 2027 and send people back to the moon in 2028. While the Artemis missions are important in their own right, NASA is also using them as a precursor to the Moon Base. That’s one of many reasons why it was a huge deal when Blue Origin’s New Glenn rocket exploded in May.

“We are building the moon base mission by mission,” NASA administrator Jared Isaacman said during Max Power. “When our astronauts return to the moon, they will arrive with the first elements of the moon base already waiting for them on the surface.”

The CLPS 2 contract is part of NASA’s Commercial Lunar Payload Services initiative, which plays a pivotal role in the moon base’s construction. The goal is to send materials and equipment to the moon ahead of time, so humans can immediately start building the base when they arrive.

The CLPS 2 contract builds on the CLPS 1.0 contract to develop the infrastructure and vehicles, including cargo landers, necessary to continuously ferry supplies and crew between Earth and the lunar surface. To fund these efforts, NASA paired the $10 billion CLPS 2 budget with an expanded CLPS 1.0 budget, which increased from $2.6 billion to $4.2 billion.

The cargo landers get cargo from space to the moon’s surface, but they also have to be transported there. NASA announced $1.3 billion in funding to Blue Origin, Arrow Science and Technology and Impulse Space to perform this step. They’ll make Orbital Transfer Vehicles, or OTVs, that act like “long-distance freight trucks” to carry the cargo landers and eventually deploy them safely to the moon’s surface.

In addition to building the vehicles that do this, NASA says three missions are slated for completion. First, research must assess how to reduce human risk and determine the best ways to deploy the CLPS landers. Second, NASA’s MoonFall drones will assess the moon and find the most optimal spot for a base. The third mission is to test the infrastructure to ensure it works as designed.

NASA took some time to talk about Promise, also known as Polar Rover for Observation, Mapping and In-Situ Exploration, an important vehicle for early moon exploration. The agency awarded Blue Origin a $94 million development contract, plus a performance-based $140 million for lunar delivery. Once deployed, the nuclear-powered rover will be managed by NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory at Caltech, which oversees NASA’s other rovers, NASA’s Deep Space Network and the Voyager probes.

Unlike solar-powered rovers, Promise’s nuclear core allows it to operate continuously through two-week lunar nights and in permanently shadowed regions where the sun never reaches. Its goal is to analyze regolith, hunt for ice and conduct general exploration.

While most of NASA’s recent announcements focused on getting hardware to the moon, the final segment laid the groundwork for surface infrastructure, specifically, long-term habitability so astronauts can actually be there.

To power this presence, NASA awarded contracts for energy storage and transmission to Honeybee Robotics (a Blue Origin company) and Yank Technologies. Their solar arrays, batteries and wireless power hardware will culminate in the Mini-Communications and Solar Tower (miniCast), assembled at NASA’s Marshall Space Flight Center in Huntsville, Alabama, before being deployed to the moon.

NASA is also trying to tackle the myriad problems that must be solved for a moon base to succeed. One of the initiatives announced at Max Power is the Moon Base Student Challenge, which asks students to help build humanity’s future on the moon, with $1 million in prizes for winners with the best ideas.

“We are inviting students from across the country to help solve some of the problems that stand between humanity and a permanent presence on another world,” Isaacman said during his speech, which was packed with students and kids, according to Space.com.

While the initiative isn’t aimed at anything specific, it challenges students to tackle whatever they can regarding lunar base problems. NASA has tapped students before to help solve problems such as dealing with lunar dust clouds and human-capable landers.
