It’s Possible to Fix the United Nations

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: It’s Possible to Fix the United Nations

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

International order is not the absence of conflict, nor is it the rigid defense of the status quo. Rather, it is the existence of predictable mechanisms to manage inevitable disagreements. And the primary mechanism tasked with upholding international peace and security—the United Nations Security Council (UNSC)—is no longer fit for purpose     .

The UNSC still resembles the world of 1945, giving privileged status to the victors of World War II and the holders of colonial empires. Without reform to give rising and middle powers a status commensurate with their growing influence, states will eventually see more sense in pursuing their security interests unilaterally or through exclusive, destabilizing blocs. For now, with a few notable exceptions, states remain committed to speaking the language of multilateralism. But make no mistake: Reforming the UNSC is not a matter of aesthetics or desirability; it is an absolute necessity.

International order is not the absence of conflict, nor is it the rigid defense of the status quo. Rather, it is the existence of predictable mechanisms to manage inevitable disagreements. And the primary mechanism tasked with upholding international peace and security—the United Nations Security Council (UNSC)—is no longer fit for purpose     .

The UNSC still resembles the world of 1945, giving privileged status to the victors of World War II and the holders of colonial empires. Without reform to give rising and middle powers a status commensurate with their growing influence, states will eventually see more sense in pursuing their security interests unilaterally or through exclusive, destabilizing blocs. For now, with a few notable exceptions, states remain committed to speaking the language of multilateralism. But make no mistake: Reforming the UNSC is not a matter of aesthetics or desirability; it is an absolute necessity.

Three dynamics have held up reform. But each may be slowly giving way, creating a rare window for genuine change.

The first and most commonly cited obstacle is the Security Council’s five permanent veto-wielding members, or P5—the United States, China, Russia, France, and the United Kingdom. The P5 countries are wary of admitting rising powers as fellow veto wielders, as it would diminish their own influence in the body and make it more susceptible to deadlock.

However, without reform, the council’s legitimacy in the eyes of the global majority will deteriorate, rendering it more and more irrelevant. The P5 will be the biggest losers if the UNSC fades into insignificance, as they have the most to lose: their veto prerogatives. Consequently, the P5 are only likely to allow for reform when the council appears      to be nearing      the brink of collapse—a point that the world may soon      approach      if the P5 continue to wield their vetoes with impunity.

The second issue is the deadlock between the two main reform groupings: the G4 coalition of Germany, Japan, Brazil, and India, which favor enlarging the permanent membership of the UNSC to include themselves, and the Uniting for Consensus group, which favors only adding additional elected seats to the council. The uncompromising competition between these two blocs has prevented other proposals from gaining steam. However, Germany’s recent failed bid for a two-year term on the council has put into question the viability of its claim to a permanent seat—and the viability of the G4 proposal as a whole. This enables a possible shake-up that allows for the emergence of new and more creative reform ideas.

The third factor is that discussions over which reform model would best enhance the UNSC’s efficiency, effectiveness, and legitimacy has, to date, been largely speculative. Neither side has been able to predict with any meaningful accuracy the implications of their proposed reforms.

This, too, is now changing. Thanks to artificial intelligence, complex simulations can be conducted that calculate potential shifts in the council’s performance. We have conducted such a simulation to determine precisely what each proposal would achieve—and what it would risk. The simulation shows that the G4 model would dramatically increase gridlock over the next five decades. Council business would slow down as decision-making speed per resolution would grow from 28.5 days to 42.1 days, and veto use would grow by more than 60 percent. Despite giving them a seat at the table, the resulting paralysis would encourage leading powers to work outside the UNSC, defeating the purpose of reform entirely.

Yet while the Uniting for Consensus group’s proposal would limit the damage to the council’s efficiency and effectiveness, it fails to resolve its legitimacy deficit. The UNSC cannot effectively serve as a long-term custodian of the international order if Britain continues to enjoy a more privileged position than India—the world’s most populous country and likely soon to be its third-largest economy.

How, then, to resolve this conundrum? The AI simulation points to two mechanisms.

First, if more countries are to gain the veto, then this must be balanced by creating a veto override mechanism: for example, a veto wielder must secure one or two concurring negative votes among sitting council members, otherwise, a two-thirds majority in the General Assembly can overturn the veto.

The simulation shows that this would reduce veto usage by up to 40 percent, even before any cast vetoes are overturned. It would also speed up decision-making on average from 28.5 days to 21 days because veto wielders would either know that they have the necessary concurring vote(s) and cast their veto without delay, or know that they don’t and be incentivized to compromise to avoid a potentially humiliating General Assembly override.

Skeptics argue that the P5 countries are unlikely to agree to any reform that dilutes their veto privileges. But the simulation projects that successful overrides would occur only 28 percent of the time (or as low as 14 percent of the time, if the vote to override takes place by public roll call rather than by secret ballot). Agreeing to this limited mechanism, which would only take effect when a great power is isolated to the point of having no allies on an expanded council, is a small price to pay to ensure the council’s long-term legitimacy—and the permanent members’ privileged position that comes with it.

Second, a pool of “semipermanent” members, serving multiple lengthy terms and rotating on and off the council a few countries at a time, would offer middle powers that fail to earn a permanent seat a substantial consolation prize. According to the simulation, it would also triple small states’ likelihood of election to an ordinary council seat by taking these middle powers out of the running.

Countries such as Japan, Brazil, Argentina, India, and Pakistan currently monopolize the council’s nonpermanent seats, serving on average more than one term per decade, leaving smaller countries to earn a place only once every 30-50 years. The simulation shows that this proposal, if tabled by a strong coalition of small and developing states, has a 78 percent chance of being adopted and contributing to breaking the logjam.

The “international order” may be an abstract concept, but it takes concrete form in the institutions through which states manage their differences, constrain the use of force, and prevent conflict from descending into chaos. None matters more than the UNSC.

For decades, the presumed trade-off between legitimacy and effectiveness has been an obstacle to meaningful reform. Our simulations suggest that this is a false choice: A more representative council can also be a more effective one. What is, in fact, impossible is a new and stable order without a Security Council designed for it.

Zachary Paikin is the deputy director of the Better Order Project at the Quincy Institute for Responsible Statecraft.

Trita Parsi is the executive vice president of the Quincy Institute for Responsible Statecraft. X: @tparsi

A Sam Altman parody offers an amusing lesson in memory management

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We verified the RAM numbers are real without sending any emails to OpenAI.

Sam Altman’s recent comments on the Cheeky Pint podcast inspired developer Iain Kay to create a satirical script that allows users to email the OpenAI CEO once per day if more than 10 percent of their system RAM is reported as available. Ram-Altman is described by its creator as a “parody project” launched in response to Altman’s viral remarks about RAM in your personal PC.

As reported by The Verge, on the October 29 episode recorded with Stripe President John Collison, Altman said, “A lot of people want local inference. And somehow, they assume that the problem is solved now because we are going to have open source models, we’re going to be able to run them on our computers, and so we’re not going to need all the stuff in the data center. But from a global allocation perspective, actually, it’s quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world’s limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5 percent of the time.”

Altman has since explained that his comments were taken out of context and briefly changed his display name to “RAM Altman” on X, which Kay calls on the project GitHub, “objectively excellent and where the project gets its name.”

In an email to Ars, Kay said, “Ram-Altman is a bash script that takes MemTotal minus MemAvailable from /proc/meminfo, so the figure includes page cache and reclaimable slab but leaves out tmpfs and shared memory.”

If the script only showed what Linux reports as “free” RAM, it would paint an incomplete picture. Linux often reports a surprisingly small amount of “free” RAM, as it doesn’t count memory that is currently claimable for use. Linux will fill available RAM caches with file data for quick access, but the kernel can reclaim that usage at a moment’s notice when needed. The MemAvailable figure is the kernel’s own estimate of how much memory it could hand over without swapping.

Users can schedule the script to ask the kernel for a status update every five minutes, and if more than 10 percent of RAM is available, it triggers a joke email from one of several joke templates. The script limits outgoing emails to one per day.

The GitHub page for the RAM-Altman project includes a dry-run option that any Linux user with a Bash terminal should be able to paste and run without the need for email trolling. When the threshold is exceeded, each dry run output features one generated joke email, so repeated runs produce different amusing examples.

A test run of the script on my Fedora 44 KDE machine confirmed the author’s measurements. Inside the System – Info Center of my Fedora desktop, the system reports 14.9 GiB of total usable system RAM after the kernel and firmware take their needed slices of the 16 GiB of physical memory. This reflects the total amount of capacity the system has to work with, not the amount available at any given time. The Ram-Altman script shows that of that 14.9 GiB total pool, 10 GiB is used, with 4.9 GiB available (33.1 percent). That figure isn’t the simple sum of the “free” and “cache” lines that the script displays, but a kernel-calculated figure that accounts for the full amount of memory that can be reclaimed.

The project concedes the nuance that Altman was referring to how much time memory spends being utilized, while the script measures how much capacity is free at the time of measurement. While the script might tell Altman that 33 percent of your system RAM is available, it doesn’t outline how often that’s the case. The concession steps on the joke slightly, but it keeps everyone honest.

The GitHub is wise to point out that “--dry-run is the best way to enjoy this. It costs nothing and all the jokes are there.” Used irresponsibly, this cheeky joke could morph into a harassment tool, and Kay quickly points out that the script does not include anyone’s email, including Altman’s. Users who deploy their own operating version are encouraged to only use their own real email addresses and honor any requests to stop, as is outlined at the bottom of the script-generated output.

If you’re interested in taking a look at some of the emails generated without having to spin up a Linux machine that reports the system status every five minutes, the ram-altman.lol project website rotates through sample emails generated by the script. Kay writes on the website that, “In fairness, he says the remarks were taken out of context, posted ‘i want you to have so much ram’, and briefly went by Ram Altman on X. That makes this script a daily progress report.”

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Report: Apple saved over $5 billion in taxes through Singapore and Ireland in 2026

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A new Financial Times report reveals how Apple saved billions in taxes in 2026 by shifting profits through Singapore and Ireland. Here are the details.

A few days ago, Apple published its annual Form 10-K, as required by the SEC, containing detailed information about its financial performance and operations for its 2026 fiscal year.

Included in the form, as the FT noted, is information regarding how Apple reduced its tax bill by billions of dollars through its operations in Singapore and Ireland.

For years, Apple’s tax arrangements in Ireland were the subject of scrutiny, culminating in a 2024 ruling by the EU’s top court that required the company to pay €13 billion in back taxes. Apple completed the payment in 2025.

That came a few years after Apple announced plans to bring most of its $252 billion in overseas cash back to the US, following tax reforms introduced during Donald Trump’s first administration.

Since then, to discourage companies from shifting profits to countries with lower tax rates, more than 130 countries have agreed to introduce a minimum corporate tax rate of 15% for large multinational companies, as part of a 2021 initiative coordinated by the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD).

Under the agreement, companies that pay less than 15% in a particular country may face additional taxes to make up the difference. The new rules also resulted in an additional $2.5 billion in taxes for Apple in Singapore in 2026, according to the company’s SEC filing.

Ireland adopted the new rules in 2024, followed by Singapore in 2025.

Back to the FT’s report, it notes that new US accounting rules now require publicly traded companies to disclose when their taxes on overseas profits differ significantly from the 21% US federal corporate tax rate.

These new requirements revealed that Apple saved $2.7 billion in taxes through Singapore in 2026, surpassing the $2.6 billion it saved through Ireland.

If Apple had been taxed on all of its global profits in 2026 at the US federal rate, it would have incurred a total bill of $33bn — demonstrating the scale of the $5bn tax reduction it received from booking profits in Ireland and Singapore. The company incurred corporation taxes totalling $27bn this year.

The FT also noted that Apple’s effective tax rate in Singapore fluctuated around 10% between 2023 and 2025, but is expected to approach 15% under the new rules. Singapore’s standard corporate tax rate is 17%.

To read the FT’s original report, follow this link.

Druski’s ‘SNL’ Gig Marks The Start Of The Creator Economy’s Franchise Era – Guest Column

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Editors note: Adam Goodman is the founder and CEO of Invisible Narratives, a creator-first entertainment studio focused on developing and scaling multi-platform franchises. He previously served as President of Paramount Pictures’ Film Group and President of Production at DreamWorks SKG.

On Saturday, Druski became the first host in Saturday Night Live history to have built his career as a social media creator. On December 1, his semi-scripted comedy Collect & Praise premieres on Netflix.

A decade ago, an Instagram sketch comedian headlining one of American comedy’s most storied institutions would have sounded like a punchline. Today, it’s a programming decision. Watch on Deadline

But the bigger story isn’t that Hollywood is embracing creators. It’s that creators are fundamentally changing how entertainment gets made.

The entertainment industry has repeatedly reinvented itself over the course of my career, from working for John Hughes while in high school in the late ’80s to my time overseeing the Motion Picture Group at Paramount Pictures and DreamWorks. From VHS to DVD to streaming, each transformation changed how content is distributed and monetized. But the fundamental process of developing content has remained largely the same.

We develop an idea, hire writers and directors, assemble a cast, finance production, and spend millions marketing the finished product. Only then do we really discover whether an audience wants it. We test and research, but by the time meaningful feedback arrives, much of the money has already been spent.

It’s an extraordinarily expensive way to find out whether something works.

When we released Paranormal Activity, we tried something different. We let audiences demand the film by city before expanding its release. Rather than simply telling audiences what to watch, we let them tell us what they wanted.

Creators increasingly operate with that kind of audience feedback built into everything they do. They start with an audience and build programming around it. They introduce characters, test formats, and learn in days what resonates, what gets shared, and what brings people back — all with a sliver of a studio’s infrastructure.

That doesn’t mean every creator can make a great movie, or that 30 million followers translates into 30 million ticket buyers. They don’t. But creators have what Hollywood has historically spent fortunes trying to acquire: a direct, ongoing relationship with an audience backed by insights and data. That audience is now watching creator content on the household’s primary screen, not just iPads and cell phones. According to Nielsen, YouTube accounted for a record 15.8% of U.S. television viewing in August, more than double Netflix’s share.

Creators are extraordinarily good at building audiences. But Hollywood has spent a century learning to build franchises.

A successful channel can reach hundreds of millions of people, but its value can depend on the creator’s ability to keep producing and satisfying algorithms they don’t control. A franchise is intellectual property that can outlive its original format, its platform, and even its creator. It’s a world audiences return to through film, television, games, consumer products, and experiences that haven’t been invented yet.

When we launched Transformers, we weren’t just making a movie about robots. We were reintroducing a world to a generation that had never played with the toys, and building something that could keep generating stories, products and audiences for decades. That’s the discipline Hollywood brings. What the creator economy has introduced is the ability to spot signals of franchise potential long before anyone commits hundreds of millions of dollars.

Five Nights at Freddy’s proves the point. The independent video game franchise spent nearly a decade building a fan community through sequels, fan theories and YouTube playthroughs before reaching theaters. By the time the movie opened, the audience wasn’t a hope. It was a fact, and the film became one of the biggest horror hits of its year. The Amazing Digital Circus offers another example: an animated YouTube series that built a global fan base entirely outside of the traditional studio system.

Creators have built something extraordinarily valuable: audiences with a genuine emotional investment in their success. Their fans aren’t just watching. They’re rooting for them. But building an audience isn’t the same as building intellectual property. Many creators remain caught on a treadmill of producing the next video, chasing trends, and figuring out what to post next week. They have loyal followers, but not necessarily repeatable formats, enduring characters, or a programming strategy. The opportunity is to turn that audience loyalty into intellectual property they can own, expand, and build into lasting franchises.

That changes the relationship between Hollywood and creators. Creators don’t need Hollywood to become famous; many already have audiences larger than television networks. What they need, if they want to build something that outlasts the next upload, is a way to turn that audience relationship into intellectual property they can own, develop and expand. Hollywood can provide the creative expertise, financing, production infrastructure, distribution and franchise-building discipline to make that happen.

Creators are extraordinarily good at building audiences. But Hollywood has spent a century learning to build franchises.

But Hollywood has to learn from creators, too. Creators have mastered continuous programming, community engagement, rapid experimentation and immediate feedback. The studios that define the next decade won’t simply adopt the creator economy’s talent. They’ll adopt its instincts while bringing the creative ambition and franchise-building expertise that Hollywood has spent a century developing.

The first chapter of the creator economy was about individuals building audiences. The second was about monetizing those audiences through advertising, sponsorships, and commerce. The next chapter will be about ownership: turning channels into programming brands and original characters and story worlds into enduring franchises.

Great stories and compelling characters have always been the foundation of this industry, and Hollywood has never had a monopoly on either. A new generation of storytellers has direct access to global audiences without waiting for Hollywood’s permission. That’s not a threat. It’s the opportunity of the decade.

The next generation of franchises won’t all come from Hollywood. But Hollywood can help make them last for generations.

Update Your iPhone to Prevent People From Seeing iMessages They Shouldn't

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Has this ever happened to you? You add someone new to an iMessage group chat, and wonder, "Huh, can they see any of the messages from before they were added?" The answer to that question is supposed to be "no." By design, new iMessage group chat members are only privy to texts that arrive after they were added. When they join, it's essentially a blank slate, as if you just created the group for the first time. At least, that's how it's intended to work.

As it turns out, there's a bug with iMessage that may allow new group chat members to see texts from before they were added to the chat. That's a problem, and not just for the iPhone users out there talking smack about people before inviting them to join the group chat. It means iMessage, and Apple's platforms in general, aren't working like they're supposed to be. You don't have to be talking behind people's backs to not want new members seeing existing chats. Maybe you're planning a surprise party for someone, and add them to the group once everyone's in on the plan; maybe the texts in the chat aren't relevant to the current group, and the person joining simply does not need to access them.

That's not all: There exists another security flaw that could allow remote hackers to join an iMessage group and receive new messages. You might add a new user to a group chat and inadvertently invite them to read all existing messages, while at the same time being attacked by a bad actor who can then intercept all future messages. Not good.

Luckily, there's currently a fix for these problems. Apple patched (and revealed) the bugs, along with seven other security flaws, with a series of software updates on Monday for iPhone, iPad, Mac, Apple Watch, and Vision Pro. Not all of the updates include patches for these two iMessage flaws—specifically, some of the updates for older Apple devices. But the rest, including iOS 27.2.1, macOS 27.2.1, macOS 26.7.3, macOS 15.8.3, watchOS 27.2.1, and visionOS 27.2.1, include the iMessage patches.

Each update includes its own list of patches, but, for this article, I'll take a look at the nine patches in iOS 27.2.1. You'll find the two iMessage bugs, plus seven other patches, in the list below:

AirPlay: Impact: An attacker in Bluetooth range may be able to bypass the rate limit on pairing code attempts. Description: A logic issue was addressed with improved state management. CVE-2026-113968: Apple

App Intents: Impact: An app may be able to access sensitive user data. Description: An authorization issue was addressed with improved state management. CVE-2026-113963: László Várady

Bluetooth: Impact: An attacker in Bluetooth range may be able to corrupt process memory. Description: A use-after-free issue was addressed with improved memory management. CVE-2026-113970: Apple

brotli: Impact: Processing maliciously crafted input may lead to memory corruption. Description: An out-of-bounds write issue was addressed with improved input validation. CVE-2026-111497

CoreUtils: Impact: A malicious Bluetooth device may be able to establish a pairing without user interaction. Description: This issue was addressed with improved checks. CVE-2026-113965: Apple

CoreUtils: Impact: An attacker in Bluetooth range may be able to bypass the rate limit on pairing code attempts and pair with a device without user interaction. Description: A logic issue was addressed with improved state management. CVE-2026-113964: an anonymous researcher

Messages: Impact: A remote attacker may be able to join an iMessage group chat and receive its messages. Description: An authorization issue was addressed with improved validation. CVE-2026-113961: an anonymous researcher

Messages: Impact: A user added to an iMessage group may be able to access messages sent before they joined. Description: This issue was addressed by removing the vulnerable code. CVE-2026-113960: an anonymous researcher

Rapportd: Impact: An attacker on the same network may be able to bypass the rate limit on AirPlay pairing code attempts. Description: A logic issue was addressed with improved state management. CVE-2026-113969: Apple

For a full accounting of the patches included in the other security updates, check out Apple's official security releases site.

To install Apple's latest security updates, you'll simply need to update your device as you usually do. On iPhone, iPad, Apple Watch, and Vision Pro, head to Settings > General > Software Update; on Mac, go to System Settings > General > Software Update. Here, follow the on-screen instructions to download and install the latest version of your device's software.

Rubio's defense of Trump's Iran war draws sharp contrast with Vance's midterm blame

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Vice President JD Vance’s post-midterm messaging broke with President Donald Trump’s foreign policy, sparking buzz about his loyalty and soul-searching in Republican Party.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio defended the Trump administration's war against Iran on Sunday, days after Vice President JD Vance blamed the conflict for Republicans' devastating midterm losses, as the White House confronts an unresolved standoff with Tehran.

"I think the president took on something that other presidents have failed to do. Sometimes, when you do the right thing, you don't know whether it's popular or not, but it's still the right thing to do. Presidents are elected to take on the hard things, sometimes difficult things, but necessary things," Rubio said Sunday.

Vance made his argument in a post on X last week.

"The reason we lost is the war. The president himself said so last night," Vance wrote last week.

"But everyone knows why we lost. And any honest conversation about where we go from here must start there," he added.

VANCE REFUSES TO PUT A CLOCK ON IRAN CONFLICT AS KEY QUESTION KEEPS US FORCES ENGAGED

Vice President JD Vance monitors U.S. military operations in Iran following an Israeli strike in Tehran on Saturday, Feb. 28, 2026. (@WhiteHouse/X)

Vance cited an unnamed pollster in the X post who said voters who had abandoned the GOP wanted the administration to end the conflict, address inflation and the cost of living, put America first and change its relationship with Israel.

The vice president's argument that the war was the primary driver of Republicans' losses, however, is complicated by polling. The Fox News Voter Poll found that 89% of voters considered the cost of living a crucial or major factor in their congressional vote, compared with 74% who said the same about the war in Iran. The findings suggest broader economic frustrations were also a major concern among voters, although the two issues may overlap and the polling does not establish which ultimately drove Republican defections.

Fox News Digital contacted Vance's office five times seeking clarification on whether ending the conflict would mean lifting the blockade, withdrawing American forces or securing new concessions from Tehran, but received no response.

The contrasting assessments come as Republicans begin looking ahead to 2028, with Trump naming Rubio as a possible presidential contender during an interview with CBS.

Some longtime Trump donors are reportedly expressing doubts about backing Vance in 2028, fearing his ties to the Trump administration and the GOP’s devastating midterm losses could hurt his presidential prospects, with some floating Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis and other Republicans as alternatives, according to The Wall Street Journal.

Meanwhile, Trump and Vance are pursuing another diplomatic opening with Iran, with Qatar mediating talks over a possible agreement that could ease the U.S. blockade and reopen the Strait of Hormuz. Trump met with Qatari Prime Minister and Foreign Minister Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdulrahman Al Thani Monday to discuss mediation efforts and potential terms for ending the conflict with Iran.

Rubio expressed skepticism Sunday that negotiations were nearing a breakthrough, arguing that Iran's demands remained unrealistic. Vance has been personally involved in indirect talks with Iranian representatives through regional mediators in recent weeks, according to Axios.

"I’m not in a political office," Rubio told reporters Sunday when asked about the midterm results. "As you know, I just don’t comment on domestic politics."

TRUMP SAYS HE DOESN’T DISAGREE WITH VANCE THAT IRAN WAR FUELED GOP LOSSES

Vice President JD Vance, center, and FBI Director Kash Patel, right, listen as Attorney General Todd Blanche speaks during an event at the FBI Field Office in Kansas City, Missouri, Monday, Sept. 14, 2026.  (AP Photo/Mark Schiefelbein, Pool)

Trump initially projected that Operation Epic Fury, launched against Iran in February, would involve four to five weeks of combat operations. More than eight months later, the broader confrontation continues despite the end of major U.S. combat operations in April.

After a June Memorandum of Understanding with Iran failed to produce a lasting resolution, the Trump administration returned to economic and military pressure, with renewed limited strikes, continued maritime tensions and an unresolved standoff over Iranian missile capabilities and the flow of oil and natural gas through the Strait of Hormuz.

Vance played a role in the administration's negotiations with Iran, helping lead diplomatic efforts that produced the June agreement before it unraveled.

Trump even joked in June that Vance would take the blame if the Iran deal failed.

"If it works out, I'm going to take the credit. If it doesn't work out, I'm blaming JD," Trump said at the time.

The vice president has previously defended the administration's military campaign, crediting U.S. forces with significantly weakening Iran's conventional military and its ability to threaten commercial shipping.

"The VP has let it be known for a few months now that he is not in full agreement with the President on Iran. But [the] social media post is striking, if not shocking," Ludovic Hood, a senior fellow at the Hudson Institute's Center for Peace and Security in the Middle East, told Fox News Digital.

Hood also questioned Vance's emphasis on the war as the primary cause of Republicans' losses, arguing that broader economic concerns played a significant role in voters' frustration.

AFTER 47 YEARS OF CONFRONTING IRAN, DOES AMERICA HAVE A STRATEGY FOR ENDING THIS WAR?

US President Donald Trump, Vice President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio (R) look on during a meeting with Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu in the Oval Office of the White House in Washington, DC, on April 7, 2025 (SAUL LOEB/AFP via Getty Images)

Trump dismissed suggestions that Vance's comments about Iran had strained their relationship on Sunday, defending Vance's remarks while suggesting he should have gone a step further in expressing support for the administration's actions.

"No, not at all," Trump said when asked whether Vance's comments had affected their relationship. "But a lot of people didn't like his comments, but not me. He was right."

Trump added that Vance "should have made one other statement," arguing that the administration's actions against Iran were worth repeating.

TRUMP'S IRAN GAMBLE DIVIDES GOP HAWKS AND 'AMERICA FIRST' CONSERVATIVES OVER WHAT VICTORY LOOKS LIKE

Vice President JD Vance speaks during a press conference following a military briefing at the Civilian Military Coordination Center on Oct. 21, 2025 in Kiryat Gat, Israel. (Nathan Howard - Pool/Getty Images)

Secretary of War Pete Hegseth also publicly defended the Iran campaign, calling it a "courageous and historic" effort even as Vance questions its political costs. Hegseth also faces questions about his future in the administration, although the president publicly insisted he intends to keep his Pentagon chief.

Beyond the political fallout, assessments differ over whether the administration has achieved its military objectives and what risks remain for the United States.

GOP CRACKS WIDEN OVER IRAN WAR AS VULNERABLE CANDIDATES BREAK WITH TRUMP, DEMAND ITS END

Vice President JD Vance walks to board Air Force Two at Joint Base Andrews, Maryland on Nov. 2, 2026. (Tom Brenner/Getty Images)

Retired Army Lt. Col. Daniel L. Davis, a veteran of four combat deployments and host of the "Daniel Davis Deep Dive" show on YouTube, offered a sharply critical assessment of the campaign's results, arguing that the administration has failed to accomplish the objectives Trump outlined when the operation began.

On Feb. 28, Trump pledged to destroy Iran's missile capabilities and production facilities, eliminate its navy and prevent Tehran's regional proxies from destabilizing the Middle East.

"Eight months and 9 days later, none of those objectives have been met," Davis told Fox News Digital.

The White House has offered a starkly different assessment. In April, the administration declared that U.S. forces had achieved the military objectives of Operation Epic Fury, pointing to the destruction of Iranian military infrastructure and significant degradation of Tehran's missile and naval capabilities.

Davis said that although American strikes inflicted significant damage on Iran's conventional military, Tehran retains the ability to threaten U.S. forces and regional allies through missiles, drones and naval assets protected in underground facilities. He warned that renewed major combat operations could expose American forces and Gulf allies to further retaliation without eliminating those capabilities.

VANCE'S SCATHING IRAN WAR POSTMORTEM PUTS POTENTIAL 2028 GOP SHOWDOWN IN FOCUS: 'EVERYONE KNOWS WHY'

Vice President JD Vance speaks during a campaign rally at Tyler Pounds Regional Airport on Oct. 13, 2026, in Tyler, Texas. Vance is in Texas to campaign for Republican candidates on the November midterm ballot. (Saul Loeb-Pool/Getty Images)

"First, it should be clear: shooting the same targets, with the same missiles, is not going to produce a different outcome," Davis said.

"Can't put lipstick on this pig: it would be ugly and seen as an admission of defeat," Davis said. "But that is still profoundly better for our interests than going back to combat ops that will only deepen, significantly, the cost of our failure."

Hood, however, rejected the suggestion that the United States should abandon pressure on Tehran, arguing that Iran continues to pose a serious threat to regional stability and American interests.

"Trump seems to get that Iran, as currently governed, is a singularly dangerous threat to the region's stability and ability to prosper; is, has long been, a source of international terrorism; and would be even more dangerous with nuclear weapons," Hood said.

Hood said the administration needs to more clearly explain its strategic objectives, including whether Washington is seeking significant concessions on Iran's nuclear program or a political shift in Tehran.

"The administration's messaging to the America people and to international partners needs improvement. What are our strategic objectives?" Hood said.

Fox News Digital reached out to the State Department for comment.

The stock-market rally keeps spreading beyond Big Tech. October inflation data could put it to the test.

Online Gaming, Crypto Payments and On-Chain Games Are Different Categories

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Uncategorized » Online Gaming, Crypto Payments and On-Chain Games Are Different Categories

Online gaming, crypto payments, and blockchain gaming are not the same thing. Learn how to distinguish payment methods, game mechanics, and on-chain execution when evaluating crypto-enabled gaming platforms.

A wallet button beside a game does not explain where its rules run. A token ticker does not reveal who maintains the game state. Even a transaction recorded on a blockchain may describe a payment rather than a move inside the game. For readers assessing gaming products, these details matter because three separate ideas are often compressed into one label: access through the internet, payment with cryptocurrency, and execution on a blockchain.

An online game uses a network connection for some part of its operation. That could involve downloading content, joining a match, checking an account, or sending actions to a server. The browser or installed app supplies the interface, while processing can be divided between the player’s device and remote systems.

None of those arrangements requires a blockchain. A server can maintain account balances and game records in an ordinary database. A browser can display animation produced locally while showing information returned by that server. The visual experience alone reveals little about the underlying architecture.

This is also why an app download and browser access do not define opposing financial models. Either interface can connect to a conventional payment system, a crypto payment service, or a mixture of both. Distribution tells you how the product reaches the player. It does not identify the system that records ownership or enforces its rules.

DailyCoin’s analysis of DeFi front ends and smart contracts examines the separation between a website and the programs it accesses. The same distinction helps when assessing a game: its interface and the system executing its rules need to be identified separately.

Accepting cryptocurrency adds a payment method. A service might receive an asset directly or work through a payment processor that monitors transfers and updates the service’s account records. The asset received and the balance displayed to the customer need not be represented in the same way.

For example, a hypothetical platform could accept a token transfer and then record a corresponding account credit in its own database. Subsequent game actions could take place entirely within that platform’s existing infrastructure. The blockchain transfer and the account credit would be connected operationally, but they would remain separate records.

A transaction hash can help identify the transfer. It does not, by itself, reveal the logic used to calculate an unrelated game result. To understand the product, the next question is what happened after payment: was only a balance updated, or did a transaction call code that changed the game’s state?

Casino catalogs illustrate why these labels need to stay precise. Slots are identified by their reel or grid layouts, symbols, paylines, and feature rules. Classic slots, video slots, and progressive jackpot formats describe aspects of the game or prize structure. Those descriptions do not specify a blockchain network.

Consider how little a slot’s format tells us about its payment infrastructure. A payline defines which symbol positions form a combination, and a bonus rule defines what triggers an additional feature. Neither tells us how a deposit reaches the account or where the outcome calculation runs. Spin Casino’s description of online slots in Canada focuses on those game formats and mechanics. It belongs to the product-description layer of the analysis, separate from evidence about a wallet integration or a blockchain contract.

That separation prevents a familiar classification error: treating a digital casino game as an on-chain game simply because both are discussed alongside cryptocurrency. To establish a blockchain connection, identify the actual payment flow, asset record or executed game action. The slot label alone supplies none of those details.

The same discipline applies to a product described as a blockchain game. That label could refer to tokenized items while the match itself runs on a server. An ownership record and a record of the rules being executed are different kinds of evidence.

To understand on-chain gameplay, look for the programs that govern actions and store their consequences. Ethereum’s introduction to smart contracts describes contracts as code and state at an address on the blockchain. Transactions can call their functions, and execution follows the programmed rules.

In a hypothetical turn-based game, a contract could check whose turn it is, determine whether a move is permitted, and update the board. Recording only a collectible associated with that game would be a narrower use of the technology. The board and its rules might still be managed elsewhere.

Products can also combine these approaches. Some assets or actions may be recorded on-chain while other systems remain on servers. The interface can stay off-chain even when the underlying game logic runs in contracts. A claim of fully on-chain gameplay therefore needs a clear account of which rules and state are included, plus any outside data on which they depend.

When reading a product description, follow one action from start to finish. Identify what the player submits, which system processes it, and where the resulting change is recorded. That gives “online,” “crypto-enabled,” and “on-chain” concrete meanings that a logo, wallet connection, or token name cannot supply.

IBM plans to bring Anthropic’s Claude Mythos to enterprise customers

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mythos would hunt for unknown flaws in customers' code, and developers would approve every fix.

IBM’s office in Dubai Internet City, Dubai, 3 November 2026

IBM plans to add Anthropic’s Claude Mythos to Concert Protect so customers can scan their own code for unknown flaws, test which are exploitable and get fixes for developers to approve. It is aiming for early 2027. Mythos itself remains limited to vetted organisations.

IBM plans to build Anthropic’s Claude Mythos into its Concert Protect security software, the company said on Monday. Mythos is so good at finding security flaws that only vetted groups can use it. IBM wants to offer the feature in early 2027.

Mythos would scan code that customers pick for flaws no one knew about, including ones that normal scanners miss. Concert Protect would then check whether hackers could really use each flaw in that customer’s systems.

IBM and Red Hat tools would draft fixes, which developers must review and approve before they go in. IBM cautioned that its plans could change. It is not betting on one lab: in June it joined OpenAI’s cyber programme for a similar service.

“The bottleneck is no longer finding vulnerabilities. It is determining which can actually be exploited and fixing them before attackers do,” IBM wrote.

That gap is real. Project Glasswing, Anthropic’s restricted Mythos programme, flagged more than 10,000 possible serious flaws in its first month, but only 97 had been patched by then.

IBM says its past Mythos work, through Glasswing, was internal research. This is its first step toward selling it to customers. That fits Anthropic’s plan to put Mythos inside partners’ products, where users get findings and fixes rather than the model itself.

Access to Mythos is tightly controlled. Anthropic briefly switched it off in June to comply with US export controls, and the White House now reportedly decides who joins its partner list. Anthropic released Mythos 5.2 on Monday, still only for vetted organisations.

‘One of the highest-stress occupations’: Why $148,000 median pay can’t fix the air traffic controller shortage

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The U.S. has about 6% fewer air traffic controllers than it did a decade ago, even as the number of flights relying on the air traffic control system has grown 10%, according to a January analysis from the U.S. Government Accountability Office. The shortage isn’t confined to American airspace.

Countries from Australia to Qatar now compete for the same small pool of certified controllers, recruiting across borders with work visas and pay pegged to experience. Aviation training company CAE projects that 71,000 new air traffic controllers will be needed worldwide by 2034, or roughly 7,000 a year.

The U.S. can’t join that bidding war. Every FAA controller must be an American citizen, leaving the agency dependent on a domestic pipeline that turns away the vast majority of applicants.

For airlines, that pipeline sets the pace of the whole system. Delta CEO Ed Bastian told Fortune in April that the biggest opportunity for AI in aviation is in air traffic control, not inside the plane, because it would “do more in terms of helping our customers have quicker travel, more efficient travel.”

The GAO traces much of the decline to disruptions in training. Government shutdowns in 2013 and in 2018 and 2019 froze hiring and training, and the COVID-19 pandemic halted training for four months, then held it at reduced capacity for nearly two years. High attrition from 2019 through 2024 made it harder to catch up.

The 2025 shutdown made things worse: by November of that year, the FAA said nearly half of major air traffic control facilities were short-staffed as controllers worked without pay.

The deeper problem may be the job itself. “It’s one of the highest stress occupations out there,” says Tiffany Bisbey, an assistant professor of industrial/organizational psychology at George Washington University. When controllers show up in academic research, she says, it’s usually as the subjects of studies on stress.

The federal government says as much. The Bureau of Labor Statistics describes the work environment this way: “Their work can be stressful because maximum concentration is required at all times. Night, weekend, and rotating shifts are common.”

Money is not what keeps people away. The median American air traffic controller earned $148,080 a year, or $71.19 an hour, as of May 2025, according to the BLS, and the top 10% earned more than $215,610.

The bar to apply is also lower than many people assume. The FAA requires a bachelor’s degree, one year of progressively responsible work experience, or a combination of the two, and new hires then spend more than a year training on the job. When the FAA pitched the job to Gen Z video gamers this spring, it advertised pay of at least $155,000 after three years.

Other countries are short too, and some are filling the gap by hiring controllers trained elsewhere. In September, Airservices Australia posted a call for experienced international controllers, offering a work visa with eligibility to transition to permanent residency and promising “the perfect balance between work and leisure.”

Australia needs the help. That same month, unplanned absences left eight of Sydney Airport’s 49 controllers unavailable and delayed roughly a third of inbound flights one morning.

The competition cuts both ways. Qatar has been recruiting Airservices Australia controllers, Canada’s NAV CANADA used an outside firm to recruit eight foreign controllers, and South Africa lost 28 controllers in eight months, Reuters reported this month. The country that trains a controller usually bears the full cost of that training, even if the controller later leaves, a spokesperson for the industry group CANSO told the news service.

The FAA’s qualification rules narrow the pool well before training begins. Applicants must be U.S. citizens under 31 and pass pre-employment tests, a medical exam, and a security investigation. “Less than 10% of all applicants meet these requirements and are accepted into the training program,” the FAA says.

Even fewer finish. About 2% of applicants qualify for and complete the full training process, the GAO found.

The agency is hiring faster than it has in years. The FAA hired 2,235 controller trainees in fiscal 2026, the most in its history, and its 2026-2028 workforce plan puts the certified workforce at about 11,000 as of April, against a target of 12,563.

That target is new. In May, the FAA lowered its staffing goal by about 2,000 controllers, citing forecast demand and changes to scheduling and training. The National Air Traffic Controllers Association said it was not involved in drafting the plan.

Bisbey sees no safe shortcut. Fast-tracking applicants or compressing training, she says, is not a viable fix, and she suspects the people who designed controller training “probably thought they needed to do even more, not less.” As she puts it: “It’s such a high-risk organization, and the training is so arduous for a reason.”

The FAA has experimented at the margins, including a 2024 pilot program that let graduates of two Oklahoma schools skip additional FAA Academy training and go straight to facility training. But certifying a controller still takes years, and until the pipeline catches up, travelers should expect more delays.

Bisbey thinks most will take them in stride, since few would trade a delay for a less safe flight. “As annoying as it is, it’s not going to make people stop traveling.”

As Nvidia probes Jensen Huang affair, here's the board member likely to play a big role

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is facing an internal investigation after admitting to an affair with a Chinese businesswoman who U.S. intelligence agencies believe is a Chinese spy.

While the company has not yet named specific investigators, that probe is being helmed by the board with help from outside counsel, according to Nvidia. Lawyer Stephen Neal is the board's lead independent director and head of corporate governance, likely making him a key figure in the investigation.

In a statement, Nvidia's board said it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised," though it will examine all evidence. China has dismissed the espionage claims. Huang, one of the most powerful businessmen in the artificial intelligence boom, said he would "cooperate fully" with the process.

Yet the probe has sparked uncertainty around Huang's future at the chipmaker and raised concerns of broader ripple effects to companies in the tech sector that are exposed to the AI build-out.

In a Monday note, Citi analysts wrote that one of the key issues for investors moving forward will be "potential governance implications" and "increased regulatory scrutiny" as the company navigates the investigation.

Nvidia's board has 10 independent members, including a former president of Taco Bell, a former Eli Lilly executive and a former vice chairman from Goldman Sachs. Neal, who joined Nvidia's board of directors in 2019, is a chairman emeritus and senior counsel of the law firm Cooley LLP, where he also served as CEO from 2001 to 2008.

Neal did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

It won't be his first corporate governance probe. According to Cooley, Neal has litigated cases across securities, bankruptcies, antitrust, takeovers and more. And he's conducted several investigations for multiple boards of directors, including at General Motors, where he advised the company in disputes with Ross Perot.

He also guided Walter Hewlett and the Hewlett Trust to stop a merger between Hewlett-Packard Co. and Compaq Computer Corp. and helped PacifiCare Health Systems with transactional matters.

According to the firm, Neal has conducted several investigations for committees of directors and independent directors, though most of those client names are confidential. He's also handled investigations with the Securities and Exchange Commission for both corporations and individuals.

When he joined Nvidia, Neal was also the chairman of the board of directors at Levi Strauss & Co. He stepped down in 2021 after reaching the mandatory board retirement age of 72, the company said at the time. The retailer did not immediately respond to a request for comment from CNBC on Monday.

Cooley has a long history with Nvidia, too. The law firm said it incorporated the company in 1993 and has served as its principal outside corporate and litigation counsel for roughly three decades. Cooley handled Nvidia's initial public offering in 1999.

Investors will be closely watching the investigation, and its emphasis on independence, as the process unfolds, according to Brayden King, a professor of management and organizations at Northwestern University's Kellogg School of Management.

"You definitely want somebody leading it who people can look at and say, 'OK, we believe this is a report that we can fully believe and trust that the outcome is the right outcome,'" King told CNBC. "That's what investors are looking for. They just don't like the instability that comes with all of this."

King said the Nvidia investigation lies at a unique intersection of Wall Street and Washington, D.C., where the company is likely navigating its next steps to try to appease both groups.

Because Nvidia's reputation is highly correlated with Huang's reputation, King said, conducting a transparent investigation will be key to regaining and retaining shareholders' trust. Not having an immediate CEO succession plan in place is only impeding that confidence, he added.

"I think the board realizes that they're doing an independent investigation in part because they really need to understand to what extent there are real problems here, and they also know that if they don't do an independent investigation, then there will be lingering concerns about whether their key stakeholders can trust the company," King said.

If the investigation shows that national security was indeed put at risk, King said, the company will need to take "very strong measures" to restore its reputation.

"That will basically come down to having to put structures in place in corporate governance to assure that this can't happen again," King said. "The independent review is going to have to be truly independent, and it's going to have to be candid enough that people believe that we can restore some normalcy around how the company operates."

It's also likely influencing how other tech companies, particularly those in the AI space, are thinking about their own corporate governance structures and how they might navigate increased scrutiny on their leaders, he added.

Marcel Kahan, a professor of law at New York University, said he's not surprised that Nvidia has been potentially exposed to industrial espionage.

Because the company operates in such a high-visibility and crucial sector, Kahan said companies like Nvidia have to be careful in their operations and whom they trust with their information. The investigation could mean the company may consider adding additional governance committees to ensure this doesn't happen again.

The revelations about its CEO will also likely mean more specialized training from Nvidia and its law firms to ensure trade secrets are kept close.

He also said the company is likely prioritizing an in-depth investigation over transparency to investors, focusing on recruiting "the best of the best" to assist with the probe.

"Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies on Wall Street," Kahan told CNBC. "I think, given all the attention that's being paid to it, this is one of the instances where I would not be that worried about anything being done in an underhanded way."

Still, although Nvidia is a major company with massive influence on the market, the controversy may not end up making too large of a dent in corporate governance structures, according to Kathryn Harrigan, a professor of business leadership at Columbia Business School.

Harrigan said because Wall Street and Silicon Valley have seen similar controversies play out before, this scandal may simply be following the script of its predecessors.

"Is it going to change the basic structure of the AI build-out? Probably not," she told CNBC. "You sometimes wait years to get on their schedule if you have an order for chips. It's not like you can magically switch from one vendor to another."

Scientists Discover a Hidden Weakness in Prostate Cancer That Could Transform Treatment

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A newly identified vulnerability in a cancer-driving protein could pave the way for a different approach to treating prostate cancer.

Researchers at the University of Michigan have identified a potential way to target a protein that helps drive prostate cancer but has long been considered beyond the reach of drugs. Their experimental compound, PBITE-1, interfered with the protein’s cancer-promoting activity in laboratory models, offering a possible starting point for more targeted treatments.

The protein, known as ERG, becomes abnormally active when two genes, TMPRSS2 and ERG, fuse together. This genetic rearrangement occurs in roughly half of prostate cancer cases among patients of European ancestry and promotes tumor cell growth and migration.

For years, ERG has been considered “undruggable” because it appeared to lack the molecular pockets where medications typically attach. In the new study, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, researchers discovered an accessible pocket in a region called the PNT domain, which helps regulate ERG’s interactions with other proteins.

After screening more than 1,600 compounds, the team identified a promising molecule and refined it into PBITE-1. In experiments involving prostate cancer cell lines and laboratory models of human and mouse prostate tissue, the compound inhibited ERG activity, triggered cancer cell death, and reduced the ability of cancer cells to invade surrounding tissue.

“Although we were the first to identify the gene fusion as a driver of prostate cancer, it was challenging to find a drug target for ERG,” said Arul Chinnaiyan, M.D., Ph.D., S.P. Hicks Professor of Pathology and member of the Rogel Cancer Center.

Most existing prostate cancer therapies focus on androgen receptors, proteins that respond to hormones such as testosterone. These receptors can activate the TMPRSS2-ERG fusion, helping fuel tumor growth. Drugs that block androgen signaling can slow the disease, but tumors often develop resistance, and treatment can cause side effects that affect quality of life.

Targeting ERG directly could provide an alternative approach for patients whose tumors carry the gene fusion, rather than relying exclusively on treatments that interfere with hormone signaling.

“This work shows that instead of treating all prostate cancer as one disease, we have the potential to personalize therapy for subtypes of prostate cancer.”

PBITE-1 remains an experimental research tool, and its effects have not been established in patients. Further research will be needed to determine whether compounds targeting ERG can be developed into safe and effective medicines.

“Although it is unlikely that PBITE-1 is ready to become a drug any time soon, this study shows that ERG is a druggable target,” Chinnaiyan said.

“It is exciting that we now have a path forward to target ERG in prostate cancer.”

Reference: “A ligandable PNT domain establishes ERG as a directly targetable oncogenic driver in prostate cancer” by Xiaoju Wang, Wenyan Liu, Jiehao Yang, Jean Ching-Yi Tien, Yu Chang, Rahul Mannan, Somnath Mahapatra, Yang Zhou, Lihao Gan, Xuhong Cao, Jiayi Zhou, Yuping Zhang, Sharpkate Shaker, Yichao Huang, Hang Qiao, Rudana Hamadeh, Grafton Ervine, Cynthia Wang, Fengyun Su, Rui Wang, Lanbo Xiao, Raghunath Ranga Sudharshan, Arvind Rao, Zaneta Nikolovska-Coleska, Cole Stephens, Lifeng Pan, James J. Chou, Debashish Sahu, Jeanne Stuckey, Zhen Wang, Ke Ding and Arul M. Chinnaiyan, 7 July 2026, Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. DOI: 10.1073/pnas.2537437123

Funding/disclosures: This work was funded by the Strategic Priority Research Program of the Chinese Academy of Sciences (XDB1060000), the National Natural Science Foundation of China (22521104), the National Cancer Institute Prostate SPORE Grant (2P50CA186786-11A1), the NCI Early Detection Research Network (U2C-CA271854) and the NCI Outstanding Investigator Award (R35-CA231996).

Tech transfer(s)/Conflict(s) of interest: Chinnaiyan co-founded and serves on the scientific advisory boards of LynxDx, Medsyn Pharma, NuLynx Therapeutics, and Esanik Therapeutics. He served on the scientific advisory boards or consulted for Tempus AI, EdenRoc, Belherra Therapeutics, Ascentage Pharmaceuticals, and Aurigene Oncology. Ding is an advisor for Kinoteck Therapeutics and NuLynx Therapeutics. Rao serves as a member of Voxel Analytics, LLC, consults for Tempus AI and Telperian and is a faculty advisor for TCS Ltd. and the Satish Dhawan Visiting Chair Professor at IISc Bangalore.

20 Best Netflix Series Performances Of All Time, Ranked

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There’s so much content on Netflix that it has both some of the best movies and TV shows ever made, and some of the worst. Some of the acting you’ll find in Netflix’s library is Hallmark-level tripe: Joey King in The Kissing Booth, David Spade in Father of the Year, Emma Roberts and Luke Bracey in Holidate.

But you’ll also find some of the greatest acting ever captured on film: Adam Sandler in Uncut Gems, Vanessa Kirby in Pieces of a Woman, Joel Edgerton and William H. Macy in Train Dreams. So, here are the greatest acting performances in any Netflix original series.

Since her breakout role as Eleven, Millie Bobby Brown has become one of Netflix’s go-to contract stars, and it’s easy to see why. Eleven stole the show in Stranger Things’ first season, and Brown demonstrated an impressive amount of depth and range for such a young performer in such a limiting role.

When we first met Eleven, she was completely alone and she couldn’t speak, so Brown had absolutely nothing to work with to endear us to this character, and yet she pulled it off. Eleven quickly became a beloved sci-fi icon.

Cristin Milioti was so great in Black Mirror, the show ended up giving her episode a sequel to continue her story. Nanette Cole is a young programmer whose consciousness is transferred into her boss’ twisted “It’s a Good Life”-style sociopathic fantasy world.

When she gets there, Nanette is just one of the despicable Robert Daly’s digital playthings. But by the end of her first intergalactic adventure, she’d managed to turn the tables on her oppressor and free herself from his cybernetic shackles. It was an empowering journey, and Milioti played every step of it perfectly.

When Netflix first announced its Ripley series, it didn’t seem like we needed another Ripley adaptation. We’ve seen it all before, and Matt Damon already gave an iconic turn that defined the character on the big screen in The Talented Mr. Ripley.

But Ripley ended up being a worthwhile readaptation. Not only did it have gorgeous, moody, monochromatic cinematography to make it more visually engaging than the movie; Andrew Scott did something completely different from Damon, and used the extra hours of a TV series to really explore the psyche of this curious character.

Everyone in The Crown did a great job of humanizing the royals, including everyone who played Queen Elizabeth II throughout her reign. Olivia Colman was spectacular as an older Queen Elizabeth, but Claire Foy was the one who got everyone hooked on The Crown in season 1.

Foy set the gold standard for The Crown’s ever-changing cast going forward. She embodied this regal historical figure in all her glory, but also interrogated the human being just under the surface.

Nell is the most tragic of all the Crains. She never did anything wrong, she just wanted her family to get along, and she’s the one who suffered the most from their dysfunctions. It was heartbreaking to see Violet McGraw’s young Nell go through all that trauma, but even more devastating to see those traumas sunken into Victoria Pedretti’s anxious eyes as grownup Nell.

The revelation that Nell was being haunted by her own future self all this time makes Pedretti’s performance even more impressive. She’s both the haunter and the hauntee in this chilling haunted-house tale.

Steven Yeun and Ali Wong both gave fantastic performances as the lead characters in Beef, and they both won much-deserved Emmys for them. They both committed so wholeheartedly to the petty rage that you fully believe these two people have nothing better to do than torment each other.

Wong has a slight edge over Yeun, because she had a more complicated character to play, and a tougher character to make us like. Danny is just an angry guy who’s fed up with the system, but Amy’s misery is a lot more nuanced.

Omar Sy is essentially giving a performance within a performance in Lupin. Assane Diop is a lot like Jean-Paul Belmondo’s antihero Michel from the French New Wave classic Breathless.

In Breathless, Michel emulates the hard-boiled characters of Humphrey Bogart. In Lupin, Assane emulates fictional master thief Arsène Lupin, who was created by Maurice Leblanc in the early 20th century. Sy nails that two-sided performance, capturing both the mask Assane wears and the real Assane underneath it.

A mysterious priest arrives in a remote town at the beginning of Mike Flanagan’s deeply personal horror opus Midnight Mass. Hamish Linklater’s subtly frightening, yet undeniably charismatic portrayal of this God-fearing eccentric keeps you hooked as Midnight Mass unfurls its vampiric mystery.

In the early episodes, Linklater maintains a mesmerizing air of mystery around Father Paul Hill, so we’re not quite sure what to make of him. But, the more overtly villainous he gets, the more fun Linklater has with the character.

One of the toughest challenges an actor can take on is playing a legendary real-life figure. When an actor is cast to play John Lennon or Cleopatra or Martin Luther King, Jr. or some other historical icon, they have to look beyond the myth to the man (or woman). Pablo Escobar is both a revered folk hero who gave back to the community, and a notorious crime kingpin who ran one of the most ruthless drug cartels ever assembled.

Wagner Moura managed to find the humanity in Escobar when he played him in Narcos. He deconstructs the mythos to show us that Escobar was just a human being like anyone else.

A little over a decade after Ben Affleck’s disastrous Daredevil movie, Charlie Cox finally did the Man Without Fear justice on-screen. Netflix’s Daredevil series is both a brutal, bloody comic book actioner and a gritty, engaging prestige crime drama.

Charlie Cox ripped the Matt Murdock written by Frank Miller right off the page. The Daredevil from Miller’s comics — the brooding antihero, wracked with Catholic guilt — is the Daredevil that Cox brought to the screen. Cox was perfectly matched with a huge, intimidating Vincent D’Onofrio as his arch-nemesis, Wilson Fisk; their intense cat-and-mouse feud is comparable to Al Pacino and Robert De Niro in Heat.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

The Nintendo Switch 2 Is Actually on Sale Ahead of Black Friday

If you've been patiently waiting for the right moment to pull the trigger on the Nintendo Switch 2 after the recent release of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, this might be a good time to do so. Woot is selling a Nintendo Switch 2 for $469 (originally $499). The discount comes as the first major discount since the Sept. 1 price hike.

The new console is an upgrade in every way, as you can read in our full review of the Nintendo Switch 2. The ergonomics and design have improved, making it much better to hold and look at. There are a lot more games out for the Switch 2 now than there were at the beginning of the year. Nintendo has hinted that they're prioritizing making more classic remakes, with many more on the pipeline, so there are plenty of games to keep you busy for a while, like Donkey Kong Bananza, Kirby Air Riders, and Hyrule Warriors: Age of Imprisonment, which are already out. Johnny Depp’s 2023 Producer Thinks The Actor Is Poised For A Hollywood Comeback With Ebenezer

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

New And Recent Movies Streaming: Big Films We Can Finally Watch At Home - Updated November 2026

I Think One Wild Horse Nine Performance Is 100% Oscar-Worthy (And I’m Loving That Reviews Agree)

The Rock Movies: A List Of Upcoming Films Starring Dwayne Johnson

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The actor is starting to book gigs again.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Johnny Depp was wrapped up in legal issues and personal matters during the early 2020s, with the biggest situation being his defamation case with former wife Amber Heard. The Oscar nominee received a mostly favorable verdict in 2022 and, since then, he’s been easing his way back into the entertainment industry. On that note, his latest feature is the 2026 movie schedule entry, Ebenezer. Now, amid this phase of his career, one of Depp’s most recent producers is getting real about the prospect of his comeback.

In 2023, Depp starred in the French historical drama film Jeanne du Barry, and the film sees him play the role of Louis XV. Nellee Holmes served as one of the executive producers on that film, and she recently spoke to CNN as part of a wide ranging story on Depp. Holmes was quite complimentary of the actor, calling him a “good human being.” In the producer’s estimation, Depp has forged connections with “people who trust him” and still believe in him. As for the quality of the leading man’s work, Holmes had this to say:

You cannot say anything that makes him bad. His work is his weapon.

In terms of Jeanne du Barry, the film generated headlines several years back due to being Depp’s first post-trial movie. The biopic was directed by Maïwenn, who also co-starred alongside Depp as the titular character. Ultimately, the movie debuted at the Cannes Film Festival in May 2023, and Depp received a standing ovation. Despite that praise, Maïwenn would later claim Depp was “difficult” to work with due to creative differences and personal conduct.

All that aside, though, Holmes is of the belief that Ebenezer will mark a turning point for Depp. The film was announced back in October 2025 and, at that time, it was also confirmed that horror maven Ti West would direct it. Months ago, West praised Depp’s portrayal of the titular Ebenezer Scrooge, noting that he possesses that “cadence and that musicality to the way that he performs.”. Another bit of praise came from Depp’s co-star, Rupert Grint, who simply referred to him as “so iconic.”

This holiday flick isn’t the only production Depp has on the horizon either. He’s also set to headline Day Drinker, which is a supernatural thriller film from director Marc Webb that’s set to be released amid the 2027 movie schedule. There have also been rumblings regarding a third Pirates of the Caribbean film being in the works, thanks to comments from producer Jerry Bruckheimer. It’s unknown if Depp would star in the film, but he joked about possibly not being able to grow facial hair again should he reprise Captain Jack Sparrow.

Ironically, amid talk about Depp making a “comeback” in Hollywood, he prefers not to use that term. Depp previously admitted to being confused by the word being applied to his circumstances, because he “didn’t go anywhere” to begin with. Still, it does indeed seem that the star’s career is on an upward trajectory and, all the while, he seems to have people like Nelle Holmes and Ti West in his corner.

Check out Johnny Depp in Ebenezer when it opens in theaters on November 13. Also, be on the lookout for Day Drinker, which is set to hit cinemas on March 26, 2027.

North Korean hackers create memecoins to launder funds from Binance hack: Elliptic

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The blockchain firm traced stolen funds through six new tokens on NEAR, where hackers used liquidity pools to obscure transfers between wallets.

North Korean hackers created memecoins to launder cryptocurrency stolen in the $6.4 billion Binance hack, routing roughly $430,000 through six newly launched tokens on the NEAR blockchain, according to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

Between Nov. 5 and 7, the hackers created at least six tokens on NEAR and used liquidity pools on decentralized exchanges to move stolen funds between wallets, Elliptic said. The tokens attracted almost no outside trading and were abandoned shortly after launch.

The hackers used one set of wallets to swap stolen NEAR tokens for the new memecoins, then used another set to sell those memecoins back into the pools for NEAR. The swaps let them move funds without direct wallet-to-wallet transfers, though the transactions remained traceable onchain.

How stolen NEAR moved between wallets through liquidity pools. Source: Elliptic

Elliptic also found that five of the six token creators were funded with proceeds from earlier token sales just two to 13 minutes before the next launch, linking the tokens in a chain of transactions.

“North Korea’s launderers rarely rely on a single technique,” Elliptic co-founder Tom Robinson wrote in the report. “Instead, they layer many cheap techniques, each adding a little more delay.”

Elliptic said the same laundering technique was also used following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025, when North Korean-linked actors moved at least $24 million through a newly created token on the TRON blockchain.

At the time of Elliptic’s analysis, about 11.8 million NEAR stolen in the Binance hack remained unmoved. Elliptic valued those funds at $43.7 million.
